  • Adjust it — who should NOT restrict timing (and what they do instead). These are not "smaller window" cases; for them the timing tool is removed, not dialed down. Type 1 diabetes (and anyone on insulin or sulfonylureas) → do not impose fasting windows; prolonged fasts risk hypoglycemia and, in T1D, DKA — any change must be physician-supervised with dose adjustment, so optimize food quality and total intake instead (disease-specific targets in §09). Pregnancy → energy needs rise (+0 kcal/day in the 1st trimester, +340 kcal/day in the 2nd, +452 kcal/day in the 3rd) and lactation adds energy too (+330 kcal/day in months 0–6, +400 kcal/day in months 7–12), so a deliberate calorie/time restriction is contraindicated; eat to appetite across the day. History of an eating disorder → rule-based windows and fasting can reinstate restrict-binge cycles; use no numeric timing rule — structured, regular meals (e.g., 3 meals + 2 snacks) is the safer pattern. Frailty/sarcopenia or age >75 with low intake → narrowing the window worsens protein under-eating; widen to ≥3 evenly spaced protein meals rather than compress. Adolescents in active growth → avoid restriction entirely. For all five groups the rule is the same: keep total intake adequate and let the clock float.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

12. Supplements: What Helps, What to Skip

Bottom line: In well-nourished people most supplements don't prevent disease — but a short list genuinely matters for specific gaps (B12 if plant-based or older, vitamin D if low-sun, folic acid pre-pregnancy, iron only if deficient, algal/fish omega-3, iodine, creatine); test, don't guess, and don't megadose.

The supplement aisle is where the gap between marketing and evidence is widest. The headline finding from decades of large trials is sobering: in well-nourished people, most supplements — multivitamins, antioxidants, and the rest — do not lower the risk of death, heart disease, or cancer. The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force concluded in 2022 that there is insufficient evidence to recommend most vitamin or mineral supplements for preventing cardiovascular disease or cancer, and it recommended against beta-carotene and vitamin E for that purpose USPSTF, JAMA 2022. The governing principle is "food first, then supplement the gaps": supplements are a tool for correcting a genuine shortfall, not a shortcut to a healthy diet Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source.

That said, supplements are genuinely valuable — sometimes essential — for specific people with specific gaps. The trick is knowing which gaps are real for you: the same pill that is pointless for one person can be important for another. (A short list of who actually benefits from what is in Practical takeaways, below.)

Key evidence

The "supplement the gap" winners (strong evidence, in the right person):

  • Vitamin B12 is non-negotiable for anyone eating little or no animal food. There is no reliable plant source; deficiency causes irreversible neurological damage and macrocytic anemia. Vegans, many vegetarians, older adults (who absorb food-bound B12 poorly), and people on metformin or long-term proton-pump inhibitors should supplement or use fortified foods NIH ODS, B12.

Adjust it — baseline RDA is 2.4 mcg/day (2.6 mcg in pregnancy, 2.8 mcg in lactation), but the supplemental dose is deliberately far higher because passive absorption (the route that doesn't need intrinsic factor) captures only ≈1% of an oral dose. Scaling and dependencies: a plant-based eater with normal absorption needs roughly 50–100 mcg/day, or 1,000–2,000 mcg twice weekly to clear the RDA via passive uptake NIH ODS, B12. Age >50, metformin, or chronic PPI/H2-blocker use → impaired food-bound B12 release, so default to the same 50–500 mcg/day supplemental range rather than relying on food. Documented deficiency or malabsorption (pernicious anemia, ileal resection, bariatric surgery) → 1,000–2,000 mcg/day oral matches intramuscular injections in efficacy. There is no UL — excess is renally cleared — so erring high is cheap; the cost of underdosing is permanent nerve damage.

  • Folic acid before and in early pregnancy is one of the best-proven supplements in medicine: 400–800 mcg/day starting before conception cuts neural-tube defects substantially, which is why grain fortification was mandated in many countries USPSTF, JAMA 2023; U.S. mandatory fortification has measurably lowered neural-tube-defect prevalence CDC MMWR 2015.

Adjust it — baseline for anyone who could become pregnant is 400–800 mcg/day, ideally begun ≥1 month before conception and continued through the first trimester USPSTF, JAMA 2023. Dependencies that raise the dose: prior NTD-affected pregnancy → 4,000 mcg/day (4 mg) starting ≥1–3 months pre-conception, per CDC (note: trials show no added benefit above ~1 mg, so this is a margin-of-safety dose, not a linear one); certain anti-seizure drugs (valproate, carbamazepine) or known MTHFR concerns / pre-gestational diabetes / obesity → clinicians commonly use 1,000–4,000 mcg/day. The UL for folic acid from supplements/fortified food is 1,000 mcg/day in non-pregnant adults, so the 4 mg doses are intentional, supervised exceptions — not a default.

  • Vitamin D reliably corrects deficiency (and prevents rickets/osteomalacia). It is reasonable for people with low sun exposure, darker skin at high latitude, older or housebound adults, and documented low blood levels. But in replete adults the large VITAL trial (n≈25,871) found no reduction in cancer or cardiovascular events from 2,000 IU/day Manson et al., NEJM 2019 — so it treats a deficiency, it is not a longevity drug.

Adjust it — anchor on the blood level, not a fixed pill. The IOM/NIH RDA is 600 IU/day (15 mcg) for ages 1–70 and 800 IU/day (20 mcg) for >70, with a UL of 4,000 IU/day (100 mcg) NIH ODS, Vitamin D. Target serum 25(OH)D is ≥20 ng/mL (50 nmol/L) for bone health; <12 ng/mL is deficient, and >50 ng/mL (>60 ng/mL especially) is potentially harmful — there is no payoff to chasing high numbers. Scaling and dependencies: maintenance/mild shortfall → 1,000–2,000 IU/day (a typical over-the-counter repletion dose, as used in VITAL — though the 2024 Endocrine Society guideline recommends against routine supplementation above the 600 IU RDA for disease prevention in healthy adults aged 19–74, reserving higher empiric doses for specific higher-risk groups such as those >75, pregnancy, prediabetes, or high falls/fracture risk); documented deficiency → a clinician-supervised repletion course (e.g., 6,000 IU/day, or 50,000 IU once weekly for 8 weeks, then drop to 1,500–2,000 IU/day) Endocrine Society guideline. Modifiers that push toward the top of the maintenance range: darker skin / consistent sun avoidance / latitude >37° in winter / BMI >30 (vitamin D sequesters in fat, so obesity can need ~1.5–2× the dose to reach the same serum level). Retest in 8–12 weeks and titrate — don't keep escalating blind.

  • Iron corrects iron-deficiency anemia — common in menstruating and pregnant women, frequent blood donors, and some plant-based eaters — but should be taken only for a documented deficiency, because iron overload is harmful. Routine iron can do more harm than good in men and postmenopausal women, and is actively dangerous in undiagnosed hereditary hemochromatosis (a common inherited iron-overload disorder, especially in people of Northern European descent). Iron-containing products are also a leading cause of fatal poisoning in young children, so keep them out of reach NIH ODS, Iron.

Adjust it — the dietary RDA is 8 mg/day for adult men and postmenopausal women, 18 mg/day for menstruating women (19–50), and 27 mg/day in pregnancy; the UL is 45 mg/day NIH ODS, Iron. The trigger for supplementing, though, is a lab value, not a demographic: ferritin <30 mcg/L indicates iron deficiency and <10–15 mcg/L indicates iron-deficiency anemia. Scaling the repletion dose (elemental iron, clinician-guided): mild deficiency or maintenance → ~18–37.5 mg/day elemental (e.g., 37.5 mg from ferrous gluconate restored stores in blood donors over 24 weeks); frank anemia → typically 45–65 mg/day (often 65 mg ferrous sulfate, sometimes dosed every other day, which newer data suggest absorbs more efficiently than splitting across the day). Dependencies: plant-based diet → non-heme iron is ~2–3× less bioavailable, so pair intake with vitamin C and assume needs run ~1.8× the RDA; hemochromatosis or ferritin already >150–200 mcg/L → 0 mg, supplementation contraindicated; regular blood donation → recheck ferritin and replace only if low. Iron is the rare case where "more, just in case" is genuinely dangerous.

  • Omega-3 (EPA/DHA) from an algal or fish-oil supplement is sensible for people who eat little oily fish, and algal DHA is specifically recommended in pregnancy/lactation for those who don't eat fish. For broad cardiovascular prevention, fish-oil supplements were neutral in VITAL Manson et al., NEJM 2019; high-dose prescription EPA (icosapent ethyl) helped only a narrow high-risk, high-triglyceride, statin-treated group, and raised atrial-fibrillation hospitalization (3.1% vs 2.1%) in the REDUCE-IT trial (industry-funded: Amarin Pharma, maker of the drug) Bhatt et al., NEJM 2019.

Adjust it — baseline replacement dose for someone who eats little oily fish is ~250–500 mg/day combined EPA+DHA, the amount you'd get from the widely cited "1–2 fish servings per week" (≈250 mg/day) that anchors most national guidance NIH ODS, Omega-3. Scaling and dependencies: pregnancy/lactation without fish → add ~200–300 mg/day DHA specifically (EFSA: +100–200 mg DHA on top of the 250 mg adult baseline; low-DHA pregnancies sometimes 600–1,000 mg/day DHA+EPA from the 2nd trimester), preferably algal to avoid contaminant and methylmercury concerns; vegan → algal oil is the only practical EPA/DHA source since ALA→EPA/DHA conversion is only ~5–10% (EPA) and <0.5% (DHA), so don't count flax/chia toward this target; established CHD → the AHA suggests ~1 g/day EPA+DHA; prescription-level triglyceride lowering → 4 g/day prescription omega-3 — but that is a drug indication, not general prevention, and (per REDUCE-IT) carries a real atrial-fibrillation signal, so it is doctor-directed, not a supplement-aisle decision.

  • Iodine matters where salt isn't iodized; the RDA is 150 mcg/day for adults, rising to 220 mcg in pregnancy and 290 mcg in lactation, with a UL of 1,100 mcg/day NIH ODS, Iodine. Adjust it — if you use iodized salt or eat dairy/seafood/seaweed regularly, you likely need 0 supplemental; the people who actually need a supplement are those eating mostly non-iodized salt (most "sea salt," kosher salt, and the salt in packaged food) plus little dairy or seafood — and vegans especially, who should target the 150 mcg RDA via a measured supplement (note seaweed is wildly variable, sometimes delivering many times the UL, so it's a poor primary source). In pregnancy/lactation, many prenatal vitamins supply ~150 mcg, closing most of the gap to 220–290 mcg.
  • Calcium is best from food, with supplements reserved for those who can't meet needs. Adjust it — the RDA is 1,000 mg/day for adults 19–50 and men 51–70, rising to 1,200 mg/day for women >50 and everyone >70; the UL falls from 2,500 mg/day (≤50) to 2,000 mg/day (>50) NIH ODS, Calcium. Supplement only the gap: tally dietary calcium (≈300 mg per cup of milk/fortified soy milk or per ~1.5 oz hard cheese; ~100–200 mg per serving of leafy greens, tofu, or fortified foods) and add a supplement only to reach the RDA — for many people that's 0–500 mg/day, not the 1,000+ mg often sold. Keep single doses ≤500 mg (absorption fraction drops above that), take with food, and pair with adequate vitamin D. Because high-dose supplemental calcium is the form tied to possible cardiovascular and kidney-stone risk, food-first is the conservative default.
  • Creatine monohydrate (~3–5 g/day) is among the best-evidenced ergogenic aids for strength and lean mass, is cheap and safe in healthy people, and shows promising (not yet definitive) signals for muscle preservation in older adults ISSN position stand, JISSN 2017.

Adjust it — baseline maintenance is 3–5 g/day, every day, taken at any time (timing barely matters) ISSN position stand, JISSN 2017. Scaling and dependencies: want full muscle saturation in ~5–7 days instead of ~4 weeks → optional loading of ~0.3 g/kg/day (≈20 g split into 4 doses) for 5–7 days, then drop to maintenance — loading is purely about speed and is skippable; larger/more muscular athletes (>90–100 kg) → may need the upper end, 5–10 g/day, since the dose scales roughly with muscle mass (~0.03 g/kg/day); vegans/vegetarians → start with lower baseline stores, so they tend to see the largest response. Skip or get medical sign-off if you have significant kidney disease (creatine is safe for healthy kidneys but raises serum creatinine cosmetically, which can confuse renal tests). Buy a product that is just creatine monohydrate and ideally third-party certified; the fancier "forms" cost more without added benefit.

The "skip it" list (strong evidence of no benefit, or net harm):

  • Multivitamins do not extend life or prevent chronic disease in well-nourished adults; the large Physicians' Health Study II and COSMOS analyses found, at best, marginal effects USPSTF, JAMA 2022; the COSMOS trial (n=21,442) found no reduction in cancer or cardiovascular disease from a daily multivitamin (industry-supported: investigator-initiated grant from Mars Edge; multivitamin supplied by Pfizer Consumer Healthcare) Sesso et al., COSMOS, AJCN 2022. Adjust it — "skip" applies to the well-nourished. A standard once-daily multivitamin (roughly 100% of the RDA, not megadoses) is a reasonable low-cost insurance policy for genuinely at-risk eaters: very low total intake (<1,200 kcal/day dieting), poor diet variety, post-bariatric surgery, or food insecurity — but it does not substitute for correcting a specific measured deficiency, which needs a targeted dose (see the winners list above).
  • Beta-carotene increased lung cancer and mortality in smokers in two large trials — a landmark cautionary tale that "antioxidant = good" is false: the ATBC study found 18% higher lung-cancer incidence and 8% higher total mortality in male smokers ATBC Study Group, NEJM 1994, and CARET found a 28% rise in lung cancer and 17% rise in death in smokers and asbestos-exposed workers Omenn et al., CARET, NEJM 1996.
  • High-dose vitamin E showed no benefit and increased prostate cancer in the SELECT trial — 400 IU/day raised prostate-cancer risk by 17% in healthy men Klein et al., JAMA 2011 — and high doses are associated with higher all-cause mortality Bjelakovic et al., Cochrane 2012. For context the RDA is just 15 mg/day (≈22 IU) and the UL is 1,000 mg/day (≈1,500 IU); the harm signal is a dosing problem, not an argument against food sources like nuts and seeds.
  • High-dose antioxidant cocktails (A, E, beta-carotene) trend toward higher mortality in Cochrane meta-analyses. In the Bjelakovic Cochrane review (78 trials, ~296,000 participants), beta-carotene and vitamin E were associated with increased all-cause mortality (with a similar but weaker, dose-dependent signal for higher-dose vitamin A); the increase was significant in the fixed-effect model and in low-risk-of-bias trials, though not in the primary random-effects analysis Bjelakovic et al., Cochrane 2012.
  • Calcium supplements (as opposed to dietary calcium) are debated: some analyses link them to a modest rise in cardiovascular and kidney-stone risk, so food sources are preferred and supplements individualized NIH ODS, Calcium.

Regulation is weak. In the U.S., supplements are not pre-approved for safety or efficacy; independent testing repeatedly finds mislabeling, under- or over-dosing, and contamination. Third-party certification (USP, NSF, Informed Sport) is a reasonable filter.

What it depends on

  • Diet pattern. Vegans need B12 (and usually vitamin D, omega-3/algal DHA, iodine, and attention to iron/zinc/calcium); omnivores eating fish and varied plants need far less.
  • Life stage and sex. Pregnancy/lactation (folate, iron, iodine, choline, DHA), menstruation (iron), and older age (B12, D, protein, possibly creatine) each change the list.
  • Sun, latitude, and skin tone. These drive vitamin D need far more than diet does.
  • Medications and conditions. Metformin and PPIs lower B12; malabsorptive conditions (celiac, bariatric surgery, IBD) create multiple real deficiencies that should be supplemented and monitored.
  • Blood levels, not guesses. The strongest case for any supplement is a measured deficiency, not a symptom or a marketing claim.

Practical takeaways

  • Default to food. A varied, mostly-plant, minimally-processed diet plus adequate sun makes most supplements unnecessary for a healthy adult.
  • The short "actually worth it" list for most people who need anything — with the dose tied to the gap, not a one-size pill:
  • B12 (plant-based or age >50, on metformin/PPI): 50–100 mcg/day, or 1,000–2,000 mcg twice weekly routine; 1,000–2,000 mcg/day if deficient/malabsorbing.
  • Vitamin D (low sun, dark skin, BMI >30, or 25(OH)D <20 ng/mL): 1,000–2,000 IU/day maintenance, target serum 20–30 ng/mL, UL 4,000 IU.
  • Folic acid (could become pregnant): 400–800 mcg/day; 4,000 mcg only with a prior NTD pregnancy under medical guidance.
  • Iron (only if ferritin <30 mcg/L): ~18–37.5 mg/day elemental for mild deficiency, 45–65 mg for anemia; not at all if hemochromatosis or ferritin already high.
  • Algal/fish omega-3 (little oily fish): ~250–500 mg/day EPA+DHA; pregnancy without fish add ~200–300 mg DHA (algal).
  • Iodine (no iodized salt, low dairy/seafood, vegan): ~150 mcg/day; pregnancy/lactation 220/290 mcg.
  • Creatine (strength/lean-mass or older adults): 3–5 g/day, no testing needed.
  • Don't megadose. More is not better — fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E) and minerals (iron, selenium, zinc) have real toxicity ceilings. Adjust it — keep total intake (food + supplements) under the adult Tolerable Upper Intake Levels: vitamin A 3,000 mcg RAE (≈10,000 IU); vitamin D 4,000 IU (100 mcg); vitamin E 1,000 mg (≈1,500 IU); iron 45 mg; zinc 40 mg; selenium 400 mcg; iodine 1,100 mcg; calcium 2,000–2,500 mg; folic acid 1,000 mcg NIH ODS factsheets. Water-soluble vitamins (B12, vitamin C) are far more forgiving and largely renally cleared, but "no UL" is not "more is better." Pregnancy lowers the safe ceiling for some (notably preformed vitamin A, where >3,000 mcg RAE is teratogenic).
  • Avoid beta-carotene supplements entirely if you smoke.
  • Test, then treat. Ask your clinician for B12, vitamin D, iron/ferritin levels rather than self-prescribing a cabinet of pills.
  • Buy third-party-certified products (USP/NSF/Informed Sport) given weak regulation.
  • Treat "immune boosters," "detoxes," antioxidant blends, and most herbal megadoses as unproven until shown otherwise — and tell your doctor what you take, because some interact with medications (e.g., vitamin K with warfarin NIH ODS, Vitamin K, St. John's Wort with many drugs).

Unsettled or debated

  • Vitamin D dosing and the "optimal" blood level remain contested: as the VITAL evidence above shows, supplementation corrects deficiency but hasn't been shown to prevent disease in replete people, and the right target threshold is still debated.
  • Omega-3 for cardiovascular prevention is unsettled, for the reasons covered above: neutral in broad trials, beneficial only in a narrow high-triglyceride subgroup, and carrying a real atrial-fibrillation signal at high doses.
  • Calcium supplements and cardiovascular risk are genuinely debated (see above); the safest current read remains "get calcium from food when you can."
  • Creatine's non-muscle benefits (cognition, bone, healthy aging) are an active and promising research area, not yet settled.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

13. Sustainability, Cost, and Real-World Adherence

Bottom line: The diet best for the planet is largely the one best for you — cutting beef and lamb is the biggest single lever (far more than 'eat local'), cheap staples (legumes, oats, frozen produce) can erase the cost premium, and the pattern you'll actually keep beats the theoretically optimal one.

A diet you can't afford, don't enjoy, or won't keep is not the healthiest diet for you — no matter how it scores on paper. This section covers three real-world constraints that determine whether the "healthiest diet" is actually achievable: environmental sustainability, cost, and adherence. The encouraging news is that they mostly point in the same direction as health. The diet that's best for the planet — more plants, less red meat, minimally processed — is largely the same one that's best for human health. But the tensions (especially cost and access) are real and deserve an honest accounting.

Key evidence

Health and planetary health overlap heavily (strong). The EAT-Lancet Commission's "planetary health diet" — built independently to feed ~10 billion people within environmental limits — converged on roughly the same plate the health evidence points to: about half the plate vegetables and fruit, mostly whole grains and plant protein (legumes, nuts), modest dairy, and small amounts of fish, poultry, and red meat EAT-Lancet, Willett et al., Lancet 2019. The Commission estimated that universal adoption of this reference diet could avert roughly 11 million premature deaths a year — largely from reductions in coronary heart disease, stroke, type-2 diabetes, and some cancers EAT-Lancet, Willett et al., Lancet 2019. That headline figure is a modeling-based estimate, sensitive to its assumptions, and has been contested (see Unsettled). Separately, food-system modeling shows that dietary shifts toward more plant-based eating are essential — alongside waste reduction and farming improvements — to keep agriculture within environmental limits while still feeding a growing population Springmann et al., Nature 2018. Major health authorities echo the same direction of travel, recommending diets built on vegetables, fruits, legumes, whole grains, and nuts, with a shift away from red and processed meat WHO, Healthy diet; the convergence of health and environmental goals is now a mainstream public-health message Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source: Sustainability.

The environmental footprint of foods varies enormously — far more by food type than by transport (strong). The landmark Poore & Nemecek analysis of ~38,700 farms across 119 countries found that animal products, especially beef and lamb, produce vastly more greenhouse gas, land use, and water use per gram of protein than plant proteins. Per 100 g of protein, global-median emissions run roughly 25 kg CO₂-eq for beef (range 9–105 across producers), ~20 for lamb, ~6.5 for pork, ~4.3 for poultry, ~3.8 for eggs, ~1.6 for tofu, ~0.8 for nuts, and ~0.4 for peas — so beef is on the order of ~60× peas and ~15× tofu per gram of protein, and even beef's lowest-impact producers exceed peas (the 10th-percentile dairy-beef GHG is ~36× that of peas) Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018 (PubMed) Our World in Data, Environmental impacts of food. Crucially, what you eat dwarfs "food miles": transport is only ~5% of global food-system emissions, so "eat local" matters far less than "eat lower on the food chain" Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018. Impacts can vary as much as ~50-fold among producers of the same product, but even the lowest-impact animal products typically exceed those of plant substitutes Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018.

The biggest single lever is reducing ruminant meat (beef, lamb), not perfection (strong). Because the footprint is so concentrated in ruminants, even partial shifts — "less and better" red meat, more legumes — capture most of the environmental benefit without requiring full veganism. Poultry, eggs, and farmed mollusks sit far lower than beef; fish is mixed depending on stock and method. The EAT-Lancet reference puts total red meat at just ~14 g/day (≈7 g beef/lamb + 7 g pork — about one burger's worth per week), versus a typical US intake of roughly 50–80 g/day of red meat (unprocessed red meat alone averaged ~284 g/week ≈ 40 g/day in NHANES 2015–16, on top of processed red meat; total meat ~100–150 g/day) EAT-Lancet, Willett et al., Lancet 2019 Zeng et al., J Acad Nutr Diet 2019 (NHANES trends).

Adjust it — red-meat target. Baseline: aim for the EAT-Lancet reference of ~14 g/day of red meat (≈100 g/week, one modest serving). How it scales: the per-gram footprint is roughly linear, so emissions saved scale with grams cut — beef ≈25 kg CO₂-eq per 100 g protein vs peas ≈0.4 and tofu ≈1.6, so swapping ~1 serving (≈85 g) of beef for legumes saves on the order of 5–6 kg CO₂-eq, and the first cuts (from a high baseline) yield the most. What it depends on:

  • Pragmatic floor: you don't need 14 g to capture most of the benefit. From a ~50–80 g/day baseline, halving to ~25–40 g/day captures roughly half the diet-related footprint reduction — a realistic first target that beats an abandoned 14 g goal.
  • Ruminant vs other: beef and lamb dominate, so cut those first — replacing only beef/lamb (keeping poultry/eggs/pork) still removes most of the excess, since poultry (~4.3) and pork (~6.5 kg CO₂-eq/100 g protein) are ~4–6× lower than beef.
  • Micronutrient risk → keep a small floor: menstruating or pregnant women, young children, and iron-deficient settings should not drop to ~0 without replacement; keep ~1–2 servings/week of red meat or substitute iron/B12/zinc from legumes + vitamin C, fortified foods, fish, or eggs (see Section 14).
  • Health vs environment weighting: if you weight environment, push toward 14 g; if you weight the contested human-health evidence for unprocessed red meat (Section 14), ~35–70 g/day of unprocessed — with near-zero processed red meat, which carries firmer harm signals (Section 05/09) — is defensible.

Cost is the genuine tension (mixed/strong). Healthier diets can cost more up front: a systematic review and meta-analysis estimated the healthiest dietary patterns cost about $1.48 more per day (95% CI $1.01–$1.95; $1.54 per 2,000 kcal) than the least healthy ones, in 2011 international dollars, with the gap driven mostly by the meat/protein group (~$0.29 more per serving) Rao et al., BMJ Open 2013. The EAT-Lancet diet's cheapest version cost a global median $2.84/day (2011 prices) and exceeded household per-capita income for at least 1.58 billion people — but this is a poverty problem, not a food-price problem: that diet was just 6.1% of mean income in high-income countries vs 89.1% in low-income countries, and most affected people (about 80%, ~1.26 billion) actually live in middle-income countries Hirvonen et al., Lancet Global Health 2020. The cheapest healthy staples — dried legumes, whole grains, oats, eggs, frozen and canned vegetables, peanut butter, seasonal produce — are among the least expensive calories and protein available. Plant-protein-forward eating can be cheaper than meat-heavy eating; the cost penalty comes mostly from fresh fish, nuts, berries, and lean meats.

Adjust it — the healthy-eating cost premium. Baseline: budget for roughly +$1.50/person/day (≈$45/month, $550/year) if you simply add fresh fish, nuts, and berries on top of a typical diet. How it scales: the premium is almost entirely the high-cost items — it shrinks toward $0, or goes negative, as you shift protein from meat to legumes (dried beans/lentils deliver protein at a fraction of meat's cost per gram) and from fresh to frozen/canned produce. What it depends on:

  • Protein source: every ~85 g serving of beef swapped for ~¾ cup cooked lentils both cuts cost and removes ~5–6 kg CO₂-eq — the cheapest move is also the greenest.
  • Fresh vs frozen/canned: frozen and canned produce run materially cheaper than fresh out of season with comparable nutrients (see takeaways), so leaning on them can erase most of the produce premium.
  • Which extras you keep: if you retain fresh wild fish (~2 servings/week), tree nuts (~30 g/day), and berries, expect to keep most of the ~$1.50/day; drop or downgrade them (tinned fish, peanut butter, frozen berries) and the premium roughly halves.
  • Income context: in the US the +$1.50/day is ~1–3% of median per-capita income; the affordability crisis is concentrated where that same plate is a large share of income, which is a policy problem, not an individual-willpower one.

Adherence and sustainability-of-habit are themselves determinants of success (strong). The DIETFITS trial showed that low-fat and low-carb diets produced similar weight loss over a year, with adherence and diet quality mattering more than the macro split Gardner et al., JAMA 2018. A pattern that fits your culture, schedule, budget, and palate is one you'll actually keep — and consistency over years beats optimization over weeks.

Food access and equity shape what's possible (strong, structural). "Food deserts" — low-income areas where residents live far from a supermarket selling affordable, nutritious food — and "food swamps" (areas saturated with cheap ultra-processed options), time poverty, lack of kitchen access or cooking skills, and food marketing all constrain choice USDA ERS, Food Access Research Atlas. These are not personal failings; they're structural barriers that individual willpower can't fully overcome, which is why policy (fortification, reformulation, labeling, subsidies) does so much of the heavy lifting in population health.

What it depends on

  • Which goal you weight. If you include environmental impact in "healthy," ruminant red meat moves higher on the limit list; if you optimize for cost, legumes and grains rise; if you optimize for adherence, cultural fit dominates.
  • Where you live and your income. Affordability and availability of fresh produce, fish, and nuts vary enormously by country, region, and season.
  • Your time and skills. Cooking from whole ingredients is cheaper and healthier but costs time; batch cooking and simple staples close much of that gap.
  • Cultural and personal preference. Every cuisine on earth has a healthy, plant-forward, minimally-processed version — the right diet adapts the core to your food culture rather than replacing it.

Practical takeaways

  • The single biggest sustainability win is eating less beef and lamb — shift some of it to legumes, poultry, eggs, or fish. You don't have to be vegan to capture most of the benefit Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018. A concrete adjustable target: EAT-Lancet reference is ~14 g/day of red meat, but halving a typical ~50–80 g/day intake to ~25–40 g/day already captures roughly half the footprint reduction and is far more sustainable as a habit; cut beef and lamb first (they're ~4–6× more emitting per gram protein than poultry or pork). If you're at risk of iron, B12, or zinc shortfall — e.g., menstruating or pregnant women, young children, or some low-resource settings — keep a small floor (~1–2 servings/week) or replace those nutrients when you cut red meat hard (legumes paired with vitamin C, fortified foods, fish/eggs, or supplementation; see Section 14).
  • "Eat lower on the food chain" beats "eat local" for environmental impact; don't agonize over food miles.
  • Eat healthily on a budget with the cheap staples: dried/canned beans and lentils, oats and whole grains, eggs, frozen vegetables and fruit (which retain most of their nutrients because they're frozen soon after harvest — vitamin content is comparable to, and occasionally higher than, fresh) Bouzari et al., J Agric Food Chem 2015, canned vegetables and fruit (convenient and shelf-stable; choose low- or no-salt-added versions, as canning can reduce some heat- and water-soluble vitamins), seasonal produce, peanut butter, tinned fish. Buy in bulk; cook in batches.
  • Reduce food waste — it's both an environmental and a financial win; plan, store, and use leftovers. In the US, over a third of food produced is never eaten, and that waste carries large greenhouse-gas, water, and land costs US EPA, From Farm to Kitchen: The Environmental Impacts of US Food Waste. Adjust it: household food waste costs the average US consumer about $728/person/year (~$2,913 for a household of four, ~11% of food spending) US EPA, Estimating the Cost of Food Waste to American Consumers, 2025 — so cutting your waste by even a third recovers on the order of $240/person/year and trims a proportional slice of your diet's footprint. The lever scales with how much you currently waste: heavy fresh-produce/leftover throwers gain most; if you already meal-plan and freeze, the marginal return is smaller.
  • Choose a pattern you'll actually keep. Adherence over years is worth more than theoretical optimality. Build from your own cuisine.
  • Expect a modest cost premium for some healthy foods (fresh fish, nuts, berries) — budget roughly +$1.50/person/day if you add them on top, but note that shifting protein from meat to legumes and from fresh to frozen/canned produce can erase or reverse that premium (see the "healthy-eating cost premium" adjust-it box above). Prioritize the high-value extras; lean on cheap staples for the bulk.

Unsettled or debated

  • EAT-Lancet's global affordability and feasibility are genuinely contested — critics argue it's unaffordable or nutritionally inadequate for the poorest and for some populations (e.g., very low red meat in iron-deficient settings) Hirvonen et al., Lancet Global Health 2020, while defenders note it's a flexible reference, not a mandate.
  • The "11 million deaths averted" figure is a contested model estimate. It is a modeling output (range ~10.8–11.6 million across three methods), and a published attempt to replicate the US mortality calculation criticized the report for poor transparency/replicability and for not fully propagating statistical uncertainty, finding flaws in the underlying assumptions Zagmutt et al., J Nutr 2020. Treat the number as directional, not precise.
  • How far to push red-meat reduction balances strong environmental evidence against weaker and contested human-health evidence for unprocessed red meat (see Section 14), and against real micronutrient roles (iron, B12, zinc) in some populations.
  • Whether "ultra-processed" should include plant-based meat alternatives is debated — some are environmentally favorable but nutritionally processed, complicating simple rules.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

14. Where the Science Is Genuinely Unsettled

Bottom line: Think in swaps, not villains — nearly every contested call (saturated fat, red meat, eggs, salt floors, seed oils) resolves into 'replace it with what?'; the foods everyone argues about matter far less than the consensus nobody serious disputes, so don't whipsaw on headlines.

A guide that pretended nutrition science was settled would be lying to you. The convergent core (Sections 04–06) is robust, but around its edges sit several debates that credentialed experts argue about in good faith — and that the headlines distort weekly. Understanding why these are unsettled is more useful than any single verdict, because it inoculates you against the next sensational study. Most of the noise comes from one underlying problem: most nutrition evidence is observational, and untangling the effect of one food from the entire lifestyle it travels with is genuinely hard.

Key evidence

Saturated fat — the diet-heart debate. Almost nobody disputes that saturated fat raises LDL cholesterol. The fight is over what it does to hard outcomes and, above all, what you replace it with. Some meta-analyses of observational data found no clear association between saturated fat and cardiovascular disease Siri-Tarino et al., AJCN 2010. But the replacement framework reconciles much of it: a Cochrane review of randomized trials found that reducing saturated fat produced a potentially important ~17% reduction in combined cardiovascular events (relative risk 0.83; moderate-certainty evidence; no clear effect on all-cause or cardiovascular mortality), with greater reductions in saturated fat producing greater reductions in events Hooper et al., Cochrane 2020. (Cochrane's own subgrouping did not separate replacement with polyunsaturated fat from replacement with carbohydrate; the case that the benefit is largest when saturated fat is replaced with polyunsaturated fat — rather than with refined carbohydrate or sugar — rests instead on the AHA's pooled read of the randomized trials Sacks et al., AHA Presidential Advisory, Circulation 2017.) The honest synthesis: swap butter and processed meat for olive oil, nuts, and fish — not for white bread and soda. This is exactly the substitution the PREDIMED randomized trial tested, where a Mediterranean diet enriched with extra-virgin olive oil or nuts cut major cardiovascular events versus a lower-fat control (hazard ratios ~0.69 and ~0.72; industry note: olive-oil and nut producers donated the trial foods, though the sponsors had no role in study design or analysis, and public funding predominated) Estruch et al., NEJM 2018.

Adjust it — saturated fat ceiling by your LDL and risk. Baseline: keep saturated fat under 10% of calories (the 2020–2025 U.S. Dietary Guidelines ceiling; ≈22 g/day on 2,000 kcal, since 10% × 2,000 = 200 kcal ÷ 9 kcal/g) DGA 2020–2025. How it scales: the ceiling is a percentage of your own intake — recompute as 0.10 × total kcal ÷ 9; and each 5% of energy you shift from saturated fat to polyunsaturated fat lowers coronary heart disease risk by ≈10% Sacks et al., AHA 2017. What it depends on:

  • Elevated LDL (≥100 mg/dL without ASCVD), or established cardiovascular disease/diabetes → tighten to <6% of calories (≈13 g/day on 2,000 kcal) — the AHA's threshold for people who need to lower LDL AHA, Saturated Fats.
  • Replacement matters more than the cap: the same gram of saturated fat removed buys an LDL drop only if it's replaced by PUFA/MUFA or whole grains, not refined starch/sugar. A worked swap: trading ~22 g/day butter-fat for olive oil and walnuts (a ~5%-energy shift on 2,000 kcal) corresponds to ≈10% lower CHD risk.
  • Normal LDL, no diabetes, no CVD → the 10% ceiling is enough; chasing <6% has little marginal benefit and is mostly about what replaces the fat, not the number itself.

Red meat — the NutriRECS firestorm. In 2019, a group published guidelines in Annals of Internal Medicine concluding adults could continue current red- and processed-meat intake because the certainty of harm was "low" Johnston et al., NutriRECS, Annals 2019. (The guideline itself reported no industry funding, but the lead author did not disclose a prior study funded by the food-industry group ILSI, which fueled the backlash.) Leading nutrition departments publicly demanded retraction. Both sides were partly right: the evidence quality for unprocessed red meat truly is low (the absolute risk changes are small and observational), yet the precautionary and environmental case for moderation is strong. Processed meat (Section 05) is a firmer "limit."

Adjust it — red-meat ceiling by goal and risk. Baseline: keep unprocessed red meat to about ≤350–500 g cooked/week (~3 modest portions) and processed meat as close to zero as is practical WCRF/AICR. How it scales: colorectal-cancer risk rises roughly ~18% per 50 g/day of processed meat (and ~17% per 100 g/day of unprocessed red meat), so cuts at the high-intake end matter most IARC, 2015. What it depends on:

  • Family history of colorectal cancer, or existing high CV risk → trend toward the low end (~≤300 g/week) and effectively no processed meat.
  • Iron-deficiency or high physical demand (pregnancy, heavy athletes, recovering anemia) → the lower bound is flexible upward; lean unprocessed red meat is a dense source of heme iron, and a few extra 100 g portions/week is a defensible trade against deficiency.
  • The number is a soft ceiling, not a hard cliff — the unprocessed-meat evidence is low-certainty, so 1–2 portions over isn't an emergency; processed meat is where the firmer "limit" sits.

Dietary cholesterol and eggs. For decades, dietary cholesterol was demonized; the 2015–2020 U.S. Dietary Guidelines dropped the strict 300 mg/day cap, concluding cholesterol in food has a weaker effect on blood cholesterol than saturated and trans fats do for most people Snetselaar et al., Nutrition Today 2021. The American Heart Association reached a similar read, advising a heart-healthy dietary pattern rather than a strict numeric cholesterol limit Carson et al., AHA Science Advisory, Circulation 2020. Egg studies still conflict — some large cohorts link higher egg/cholesterol intake to modestly higher cardiovascular risk (in the pooled U.S. cohorts, each additional 300 mg/day of dietary cholesterol — and, separately, each additional half-egg/day — tracked with ~17% higher CVD risk) Zhong et al., JAMA 2019, while updated meta-analyses pooling more cohorts find no association up to about one egg/day — and individuals vary in how much dietary cholesterol raises their blood levels ("hyper-responders"). The defensible read: an egg a day is fine for most healthy people; those with diabetes or high cardiovascular risk may want to be more cautious — though the evidence here is mixed and largely observational, so that caution is precautionary rather than firmly established.

Adjust it — eggs by your risk profile. Baseline for a healthy adult: up to 1 egg/day (~7/week) carries no detectable CVD association in pooled cohorts Drouin-Chartier et al., BMJ 2020. How it scales: in the older U.S. pooled cohorts, each +half-egg/day was associated with ~17% higher CVD risk, as was each +300 mg/day of dietary cholesterol (these were two separate exposures, not equivalents — one large egg holds ≈185 mg cholesterol, all in the yolk) Zhong et al., JAMA 2019. What it depends on:

  • Type 2 diabetes (established) → cap nearer ~3–4 eggs/week. In diabetic subgroups, each additional ~4 eggs/week was associated with ~40% higher CVD risk, and each egg/day with ~13% higher incident-diabetes risk in those at risk egg dose-response meta-analysis, EJN 2021.
  • High CV risk / prior event / known hyper-responder → treat eggs as part of your saturated-fat and cholesterol budget, lean toward ≤3–4 yolks/week, and use whites freely (a 2-white omelet adds ~0 mg cholesterol).
  • Healthy, normal lipids, no diabetes → 1/day is a reasonable upper habit, not a target to hit; the protein and choline are useful, and the signal at this level is null.

Salt — the J-curve dispute. That high sodium raises blood pressure and that population-level reduction lowers it is well-supported (Sections 05–06). The clearest hard-outcome RCT is the SSaSS trial, in which switching to a potassium-enriched salt substitute cut stroke, major cardiovascular events, and death — though this tested sodium-for-potassium substitution (so the added potassium is a co-mechanism, not pure sodium reduction) in an unusually high-risk rural-Chinese population (most had prior stroke or hypertension, with very high baseline sodium) Neal et al., NEJM 2021. One caveat: potassium-based salt substitutes can be dangerous for people with chronic kidney disease or on potassium-sparing or other potassium-affecting medications, who were excluded from the trial. The controversy is at the low end: some large observational studies (notably PURE) found a J- or U-shaped curve, with very low sodium also associated with higher risk O'Donnell et al., NEJM 2014. Critics counter that those findings are plagued by reverse causation and flawed single-spot-urine estimates. Major bodies still advise most people eat too much sodium and should cut down — the WHO recommends adults keep sodium below 2 g/day (about 5 g/day of salt), well under typical intake WHO 2023; the debate is whether there's a floor below which cutting further stops helping.

Adjust it — sodium target by your blood pressure and risk. Baseline for most adults: aim below 2,300 mg/day (the AHA's everyday target and ~1 tsp salt), versus a typical U.S. intake of ~3,400 mg AHA, How Much Sodium. How it scales: in DASH-Sodium, cutting sodium lowered systolic blood pressure by roughly 3–8 mm Hg per ~1,000 mg/day reduction, with the larger drops in people who start with higher blood pressure DASH-Sodium, NEJM 2001. What it depends on:

  • Hypertensive, salt-sensitive, Black, diabetic, or age >50–51 (also CKD or prior cardiovascular event) → push toward <1,500 mg/day (the AHA "ideal"); salt-sensitivity is present in ~51% of hypertensives vs ~26% of normotensives, so these groups get the biggest BP payoff AHA, Salt Sensitivity.
  • The J-curve caveat applies mainly below ~1,500 mg/day — that is where some observational data (e.g., PURE) hint at a floor, and where confidence is genuinely low; for the ~90%+ of people eating >2,300 mg, the debate is academic and the instruction is unambiguous: cut down. Don't use the floor debate as a reason to stay at 3,400 mg.
  • Heavy sweating (athletes, hot-climate labor) → add back losses, not subtract: sweat carries ~0.5–1.5 g sodium per liter, so 2–3 L of sweat can justify replacing ~1–4 g/day on top of baseline.
  • Salt-substitute caution: potassium-based substitutes lower risk in most, but are unsafe in advanced CKD (e.g., eGFR <30 / G4–G5) or on potassium-sparing drugs, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, or other potassium-raising medication — those groups were excluded from SSaSS.

Seed oils and omega-6. A vocal claim holds that industrial seed oils (high in omega-6 linoleic acid) drive inflammation and chronic disease. The weight of randomized and prospective evidence does not support this: higher linoleic acid intake is generally associated with lower cardiovascular risk, and replacing saturated fat with these oils lowers LDL AHA Advisory, Circulation 2017. A pooled analysis of 30 cohorts found higher circulating linoleic acid biomarkers tracked with lower risk of cardiovascular disease and cardiovascular death Marklund et al., Circulation 2019. The legitimate kernel is that seed oils are abundant in ultra-processed foods — so the foods carrying them are often the problem, not the oil's fatty acids per se.

Adjust it — judge the vehicle, not the omega-6. Baseline: there is no evidence-based upper limit on linoleic acid for health; the AMDR for total omega-6 PUFA is ~5–10% of calories and most people sit comfortably inside it. How it scales: each 5%-energy swap of saturated fat to these oils lowers CHD risk ~10% (same lever as above), so the direction of adjustment is usually more liquid plant oil in cooking, not less. What it depends on:

  • High ultra-processed-food intake → the fix is cutting the UPFs (target <10–15% of calories from UPFs where feasible; §05), not banning canola in your own pan.
  • Omega-3 balance, not omega-6 ceiling, is the lever worth pulling: aim for ~250–500 mg/day EPA+DHA (oily fish ~2×/week) rather than trying to drive linoleic acid down.

Low-carb vs. low-fat. For weight loss, the best head-to-head trial (DIETFITS) found no meaningful average difference between a healthy low-carb and a healthy low-fat diet once calories and adherence were accounted for, concluding that dietary adherence — more than the specific macronutrient mix — predicted weight-loss success Gardner et al., JAMA 2018. An earlier head-to-head trial, A TO Z, did find the lowest-carb (Atkins) arm lost somewhat more weight at 12 months, but adherence was poor across all arms and the differences narrowed over time Gardner et al., A TO Z, JAMA 2007. Low-carb can offer short-term advantages for glycemic control in type 2 diabetes (Section 09), but long-term cardiovascular outcomes depend on what the carbs are replaced with — plant-based low-carb looks better than animal-based. There is no universal winner.

Adjust it — carb level by metabolic status, not ideology. Baseline: there is no single optimal carbohydrate fraction; the U.S. guidelines' acceptable range is 45–65% of calories from carbohydrate, and within it adherence beats macro-ratio. How it scales: each diet in DIETFITS averaged only a ~5–6 kg 12-month loss with no significant between-diet difference, so the lever is the ~500 kcal/day deficit (≈0.45 kg/week; see §20), not the carb percentage. What it depends on:

  • Type 2 diabetes or strong glycemic goal → a lower-carb pattern (commonly defined as <130 g/day, ~26% of a 2,000-kcal diet) can improve HbA1c short-term (§09) — a ~10–15%-of-energy reduction in carbohydrate buys roughly the ~0.5% HbA1c edge cited in §09, which largely fades by ~12 months; pair it with plant proteins and unsaturated fats, since animal-heavy low-carb tracks worse for long-term CV outcomes.
  • Endurance athlete or very active → keep carbs higher (often 5–8 g/kg/day) to fuel training; aggressive low-carb impairs high-intensity performance.
  • Whatever level you pick, fix the quality first: whole grains, legumes, fruit, and ~14 g fiber/1,000 kcal (§04) matter more than the carb number — "healthy low-carb" and "healthy low-fat" both beat their refined-food versions.

The limits of nutritional epidemiology (the meta-controversy). Most diet–disease links rest on observational cohorts using food-frequency questionnaires (imprecise self-report), confounded by the fact that health-conscious people do many healthy things at once ("healthy-user bias"), and occasionally distorted by industry funding. Critics like John Ioannidis argue these limitations make many single findings unreliable and call for major reform of the field Ioannidis, JAMA 2018. This is why this guide leans hardest on the places where randomized trials and convergent patterns agree (Mediterranean/DASH, fiber, trans fat, sodium, sugar-sweetened beverages) and treats single sensational studies with skepticism.

What it depends on

  • What the food replaces. Nearly every fat and carbohydrate debate resolves into a substitution question — saturated fat for what?, carbs for what? The replacement, not the nutrient in isolation, drives the answer.
  • Your individual physiology. Cholesterol hyper-responders, salt-sensitive individuals, and people with diabetes get different answers on eggs, salt, and carbohydrate than the average person.
  • Evidence type. "Low-certainty" (red meat, very-low salt) means be appropriately humble, not "do whatever you want." Strong RCT-backed items (trans fat, replacing saturated fat with PUFA) deserve more confidence.
  • Who funded and how it was measured. Weigh single observational studies — especially industry-funded or FFQ-based ones — far less than convergent trial evidence.

Practical takeaways

  • Don't whipsaw on headlines. A single study rarely overturns a convergent body of evidence; wait for replication and meta-analyses.
  • Think in swaps, not villains. "Replace saturated fat with olive oil, nuts, and fish" is more accurate and more useful than "fat is bad" or "fat is fine."
  • The contested foods are mostly moderation, not elimination: eggs, unprocessed red meat, and dairy can fit a healthy diet in modest amounts for most people — full-fat dairy is roughly neutral, not clearly harmful, in modest amounts (~2–3 servings/day), with fermented forms (yogurt/kefir) looking best in cohorts.
  • Trust the boring consensus. The items everyone argues about (eggs, butter, salt floors, seed oils) matter far less than the items nobody serious argues about: eat more plants and fiber, less sugar and ultra-processed food, no trans fat, don't smoke, stay active.
  • Match the contested calls to your risk — with numbers. Healthy adults: up to ~1 egg/day, red meat ≤350–500 g/week, sodium <2,300 mg, saturated fat <10% of calories. Higher-risk readers tighten each: diabetes → ~3–4 eggs/week and lower-carb; hypertension/CKD/salt-sensitive → sodium <1,500 mg; elevated LDL or established CVD → saturated fat <6% of calories. The per-condition deltas are in the "Adjust it —" boxes above.

Unsettled or debated

This entire section is the unsettled territory. The most important meta-point: where this guide expresses confidence, it's because randomized trials and multiple independent lines of evidence agree; where it hedges, the underlying science genuinely hedges. New evidence that would move these debates would be large, long-term randomized trials with hard outcomes and objective intake measures (e.g., biomarkers instead of questionnaires) — which are expensive and rare, and why these arguments will persist.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

15. Gut Health: The Microbiome, Fiber Diversity, and Fermented Foods

Bottom line: Feed your microbiome like a garden — ~30 different plants a week, fermented foods, and fiber ramped slowly — rather than buying a probiotic, which helps only for narrow, strain-specific uses (e.g., alongside antibiotics), not general 'gut health.'

Your large intestine houses trillions of bacteria, archaea, fungi, and viruses — collectively the gut microbiome — that function less like passengers and more like a metabolic organ you can feed. They digest the fiber your own enzymes cannot, train your immune system, and produce a stream of bioactive compounds that reach your blood, your fat tissue, and possibly your brain. Each person's community is nearly as individual as a fingerprint, shaped by birth, early-life exposures, antibiotics, and — the part you control — what you eat Harvard Nutrition Source, The Microbiome.

The single most important thing these microbes do for diet is ferment fiber and resistant starch into short-chain fatty acids (SCFAs) — chiefly butyrate, acetate, and propionate. Butyrate is the preferred fuel for the cells lining your colon; SCFAs also help regulate appetite hormones, blood glucose, and inflammatory signaling, and they reinforce the gut barrier that keeps bacterial products out of your bloodstream Harvard Nutrition Source, The Microbiome. This is the leading proposed mechanism connecting "eat more fiber" (§02) to outcomes far beyond digestion: metabolism, immunity, and the gut–brain axis (§18) — though how much of fiber's benefit actually runs through SCFAs is not settled.

A useful frame: you don't really "take care of your gut" with a single product. You garden it — feeding a diverse range of beneficial species with a diverse range of plants, and occasionally adding live cultures through fermented foods. The evidence below supports that gardening metaphor better than it supports any pill.

Key evidence

Plant diversity, not just plant quantity, tracks with a richer microbiome (moderate, observational). The American Gut Project — the largest citizen-science microbiome dataset, with over 15,000 samples from more than 11,000 people — found that the number of unique plant types a person ate per week predicted gut bacterial diversity better than self-labeled diets like "vegan" or "omnivore." People eating more than 30 different plant types per week had more diverse microbiomes and more SCFA-producing bacteria than those eating 10 or fewer McDonald et al., mSystems 2018. Higher diversity is generally considered a marker of a resilient, healthy gut, though this is correlational — diverse-plant eaters differ in many ways — so treat "30 plants a week" as a useful target, not a proven causal dose.

Fermented foods raised diversity and lowered inflammation in a randomized trial (moderate, but small and short). In a Stanford randomized study, 36 healthy adults followed either a high-fermented-food diet or a high-fiber diet for 10 weeks. The fermented-food arm — yogurt, kefir, fermented cottage cheese, kimchi and other fermented vegetables, vegetable-brine drinks, and kombucha — showed a steady increase in overall microbiome diversity (larger servings, larger effect) and a decrease in 19 inflammatory proteins, including interleukin-6, a cytokine tied to rheumatoid arthritis, type 2 diabetes, and chronic stress Wastyk et al., Cell 2021; Stanford Medicine 2021. The contrast is the interesting part: the high-fiber arm did not raise diversity or lower those inflammatory markers over 10 weeks — the authors suspect many participants' existing microbes lacked the enzymes to fully break down the added fiber that quickly. One crucial caveat usually lost in the headlines: the trial's pre-registered primary outcome — a composite cytokine/immune response score — did not change in either arm. The 19-marker reduction and the diversity increase were secondary findings (n=36, 10 weeks, healthy adults). That matters because secondary results, not designated in advance, carry more risk of being chance findings. The honest caveats: this was a small, short trial in healthy people, measuring biomarkers rather than disease outcomes, and its primary endpoint was null. It is a genuinely encouraging signal for fermented foods, not a settled prescription.

Prebiotics, resistant starch, and polyphenols feed the gardeners (moderate, mechanistic). Prebiotics are the fibers and carbohydrates your microbes ferment — inulin, fructooligosaccharides, pectins, and resistant starch — concentrated in garlic, onions, leeks, asparagus, slightly-green bananas, beans and legumes, oats, and cooled-then-reheated potatoes, rice, and pasta (cooling raises resistant starch) Harvard Nutrition Source, The Microbiome. Polyphenols — the colorful compounds in berries, cocoa, coffee, tea, and extra-virgin olive oil — are also metabolized by gut bacteria into beneficial products. The practical upshot is the same as §04: a wide variety of minimally processed plants does most of the work.

Probiotic supplements are strain- and indication-specific — not a general tonic (strong, against the hype). This is where marketing outruns evidence. The American Gastroenterological Association's 2020 guideline reviewed the literature and found insufficient evidence to recommend probiotics for most digestive conditions — including IBS (§09), Crohn's disease, ulcerative colitis, and treating an active C. difficile infection. It identified only a few situations with adequate support: preventing necrotizing enterocolitis in preterm low-birthweight infants, preventing antibiotic-associated C. difficile in people on antibiotics, and managing pouchitis AGA 2020; Su et al., Gastroenterology 2020. Consistent with this, Cochrane reviews find probiotics modestly reduce antibiotic-associated diarrhea in children (about one case prevented per 9 treated) Guo et al., Cochrane 2019 and C. difficile-associated diarrhea (about one per 42, and fewer — about one per 12 — in higher-risk patients) when started alongside antibiotics Goldenberg et al., Cochrane 2017. The benefit is real but narrow, dose- and strain-dependent, and does not generalize to "take a probiotic for general wellness."

Some additives appear to harm the microbiome (limited but mechanistically concerning). A double-blind controlled-feeding study had healthy adults eat an otherwise-identical diet with or without 15 g/day of the emulsifier carboxymethylcellulose (CMC) for 11 days. CMC consumption perturbed the microbiota, depleted beneficial metabolites including SCFAs, and in two participants drove bacteria into the normally sterile inner mucus layer — an early hallmark of gut inflammation Chassaing et al., Gastroenterology 2022. This is a small, short, biomarker-level study, not proof that emulsifiers cause disease — but it offers a plausible mechanism for why ultra-processed foods (§05), which are low in fiber and rich in such additives, associate with poorer gut and metabolic health.

What it depends on

  • Your starting microbiome. Two people eating identical fiber can ferment it very differently; if your community lacks certain fiber-degrading species, benefits build more slowly (the Stanford fiber arm is the cautionary tale).
  • The specific strain and dose. "Probiotic" is not one thing. Evidence for Saccharomyces boulardii or particular Lactobacillus/Bifidobacterium strains in one setting says nothing about a different product in a different setting.
  • Your health status. Probiotic supplements warrant caution in critically ill, immunocompromised, or central-line patients; benefits and risks differ from those in healthy adults.
  • Pace of change. Ramping fiber or fermented foods too fast commonly causes gas and bloating; the microbiome adapts over ~2–4 weeks, not days — which is why the practical advice below is to add roughly +5 g fiber per week (or +2–3 g/week if you bloat easily) rather than overhauling intake overnight.

Practical takeaways

  • Aim for ~30 different plant types per week. Count vegetables, fruits, whole grains, legumes, nuts, seeds, herbs, and spices. Variety beats volume of any single "superfood."
  • Adjust it — Baseline target: 30 unique plant types/week (the American Gut high-diversity threshold; the contrast group ate ≤10) McDonald et al., mSystems 2018. How it scales: the relationship is roughly monotonic, not a cliff at 30 — going from 10 → 20 → 30 each step tracks with more bacterial and metabolite diversity, so every additional distinct plant counts; the "30" is a round, memorable point on a continuum, not a validated dose. A spice (cumin, oregano), a different-colored variety (red vs. green cabbage counts as two), and a handful of mixed nuts each add to the tally. What it depends on: if you currently eat <10 types, target +5 new types/week rather than leaping to 30 (pace it with the fiber ramp below to limit gas); if you reliably hit 30, there is no evidence that pushing to 40–50 adds measurable benefit — diminishing returns, so spend the effort elsewhere. This is an observational target (the high-diversity vs. low-diversity comparison was just 41 vs. 44 people), so treat it as a low-cost habit, not a proven causal dose.
  • Consider fermented foods as a reasonable, low-risk addition. A serving or two of yogurt or kefir with live cultures, kimchi, sauerkraut (refrigerated, not shelf-stable pasteurized), miso, or kombucha. This is a low-risk bet, not a proven prescription: one small 10-week trial linked them to higher microbiome diversity and lower inflammation, with larger and more frequent servings showing bigger effects Wastyk et al., Cell 2021.
  • Adjust it — Baseline: 1–2 servings/day of live-culture fermented food (a serving ≈ ¾–1 cup / ~170 g yogurt or kefir, ½ cup / ~75 g kimchi or sauerkraut, 1 cup / 240 mL kombucha). How it scales: the only trial showing the diversity and inflammation effect moved participants from a baseline of 0.4 servings/day up to ~6 servings/day, and the effect was dose-dependent (bigger and more frequent servings → larger diversity gain) Wastyk et al., Cell 2021. So 2–4 servings/day is a defensible "more confident" target if tolerated; ~6/day is the only dose with trial data, but is a lot of food and salt for most people. What it depends on: on a sodium-restricted diet (hypertension, heart failure — target <1,500–2,300 mg sodium/day, §09), favor low-salt ferments (plain yogurt/kefir ≈ 50–150 mg sodium/serving) over kimchi/sauerkraut/miso (often 400–900 mg sodium/½-cup or per Tbsp miso) and count them toward your sodium budget; if immunocompromised, pregnant, or on a central line, prefer pasteurized-then-recultured dairy and skip raw unpasteurized kombucha/vegetable ferments (small infection risk, see "health status" above); ramp +1 serving every few days if new to ferments, since the gas/bloating caveat applies here too.
  • Feed butyrate-makers specifically: beans and lentils, oats and barley, onions/garlic/leeks, and cooled-then-reheated potatoes or rice for resistant starch.
  • Don't buy a probiotic for "gut health" in general. Reserve them for evidence-backed uses (e.g., alongside antibiotics to prevent diarrhea), and match the strain to the indication AGA 2020.
  • Adjust it — There is no general "daily probiotic" dose worth recommending; the decision is indication-specific, and the number that matters is the NNT (people you'd treat to prevent one bad outcome):
  • Child on antibiotics → consider a probiotic during the course: NNT ≈ 9 to prevent one case of antibiotic-associated diarrhea (NNT ≈ 6 at high doses ≥5 billion CFU/day) Guo et al., Cochrane 2019.
  • Adult/child on antibiotics, worried about C. difficile → NNT ≈ 42 overall, but ≈ 12 when baseline C. diff risk is >5% (older, hospitalized, prior episode) — so the higher your baseline risk, the more it's worth it Goldenberg et al., Cochrane 2017. Start it within ~2 days of the first antibiotic dose, when the benefit is concentrated.
  • Pouchitis, or preventing necrotizing enterocolitis in preterm low-birthweight infants → specific strains have adequate support AGA 2020.
  • IBS (§09), Crohn's, ulcerative colitis, treating active C. diff, or "general wellness" → NNT effectively undefined (insufficient evidence); don't spend on it. What it depends on: a result for one strain at one dose (CFU/day) in one condition does not transfer to a different product — match strain, dose, and indication, and stop when the course ends.
  • Protect what you have: eat enough fiber, minimize ultra-processed foods and emulsifier-heavy products (§05), and use antibiotics only when genuinely needed — they are sometimes life-saving but indiscriminately disrupt the community.
  • Go slow. Increase fiber and ferments gradually with adequate fluids to limit gas and bloating.
  • Adjust it — Baseline ramp: +5 g fiber per week, not all at once, toward a target of ~14 g fiber per 1,000 kcal — i.e., ~25 g/day for women and ~38 g/day for men at typical intakes (the IOM/Dietary Guidelines adequate intake; most US adults sit near ~17 g/day, so there is real room to climb) DGA / IOM, via StatPearls. How it scales: add ~240 mL (8 oz) of water per +5 g fiber to limit bloating, and expect the gut to adapt over ~2–4 weeks, not days, before adding the next increment. What it depends on: if you're fiber-naïve or get bloated easily, slow to +2–3 g/week; adults >50 have a lower target (~21 g/day women, ~30 g/day men) simply because they eat fewer calories; on very high energy intakes scale by the 14 g/1,000 kcal rule (e.g., 3,000 kcal → ~42 g/day); with a flare-prone GI condition (IBS, IBD, recent obstruction/stricture) disregard the population target and titrate with your clinician — more fiber can worsen symptoms. Calorie needs that anchor the per-1,000-kcal math come from Mifflin–St Jeor (§20).

Unsettled or debated

The biggest open question is causation versus correlation: higher microbiome diversity is a marker of health, but we cannot yet say that deliberately raising diversity prevents specific diseases — the strongest data (American Gut) are observational, and the strongest trial (Stanford) is small, short, and biomarker-based. Whether fermented foods improve hard clinical outcomes, and over what timeframe fiber reshapes a "fiber-naïve" microbiome, remain unresolved. The emulsifier signal is biologically plausible but rests on small human studies plus animal work, so regulators have not declared specific additives unsafe. And the gut–brain link (§18) — SCFAs and microbial metabolites influencing mood and cognition — is among the most hyped and least settled areas in all of nutrition: promising mechanisms, thin human outcome data. The defensible stance is to do the high-confidence things (diverse plants, fiber, fermented foods, fewer ultra-processed additives) that are beneficial regardless of how these debates resolve.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

16. Diet Across the Lifespan: Infancy, Childhood, Pregnancy, and Healthy Aging

Bottom line: The same core holds at every age, but the edges of life shift the rules qualitatively — and the highest-stakes windows are the shortest (folic acid before you know you're pregnant, early allergen introduction at 4-11 months, bone built by the early 20s, more protein after ~65).

The shared core described throughout this guide — mostly minimally-processed plants, adequate protein, unsaturated fats, little added sugar or sodium — holds at every age. But life stage is the single largest legitimate modifier of that core (§08), and at the two ends of life it shifts requirements not just by dose but qualitatively: a six-month-old, a pregnant adult, and an eighty-year-old have genuinely different nutritional rules, not just different portion sizes. This section puts numbers on those rules.

Two themes recur. First, the highest-stakes windows are the shortest: the folic-acid window closes before most people know they're pregnant, the early-allergen window is roughly months 4–11, and peak bone mass is mostly banked by the early twenties. Second, "more" and "less" both matter at the edges — infants and elders are vulnerable to deficiency (iron, vitamin D, B12, protein) in a way healthy adults rarely are, while childhood is where lifelong patterns around sugar and around food-as-control are set.

This deepens §08's life-stage overview. It cross-references micronutrients (§03), supplements (§12), weight change (§10), and special diets (§17). Where I cite a single number, treat it as a guideline target, not a personalized prescription — clinical situations vary.

Key evidence

Infancy — about 6 months exclusive breastfeeding, then complementary foods, with early allergen introduction (strong). WHO and the AAP recommend roughly 6 months of exclusive breastfeeding (or formula), then introducing complementary foods while continuing milk feeds WHO, Breastfeeding. The timing of allergenic foods was reversed by trial evidence: the LEAP RCT found early introduction of peanut (age 4–11 months) cut peanut allergy by 81% (intention-to-treat) by age 5 in high-risk infants — a 13.7% vs 1.9% absolute split (avoidance vs early-introduction) in the no-prior-sensitization stratum Du Toit/LEAP, NEJM 2015. The EAT trial introduced six allergens at 3 months in a general-population cohort; it missed its intention-to-treat endpoint but established that early introduction is safe, and per-protocol analyses favored early egg and peanut Perkin/EAT, NEJM 2016. NIAID's 2017 risk-stratified guidance is the practical playbook: introduce peanut early for low-risk babies at home, but for high-risk infants (severe eczema and/or egg allergy) test peanut-specific IgE or skin-prick first, sometimes with a supervised first feeding NIAID 2017 Addendum Guidelines. Two hard rules: no honey before 12 months (infant botulism risk; spores survive cooking) and no added sugar in early childhood — the 2025–2030 Dietary Guidelines (released January 2026) now advise avoiding added sugars until about age 10, a jump from the 2020–2025 "before age 2" line USDA/HHS, Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2025–2030; CDC, Foods and Drinks to Avoid or Limit. Two micronutrients need active attention: breastfed infants should get 400 IU/day (10 mcg) vitamin D from the first days of life, and fetal iron stores — laid down mostly in the third trimester — run low around 4–6 months, so the AAP recommends exclusively/partially breastfed term infants get ~1 mg/kg/day iron starting at 4 months until iron-rich complementary foods (fortified cereals, pureed meats, legumes) take over AAP/HealthyChildren, Vitamin D & Iron Supplements.

Adjust it — infant supplements scale with feeding mode, intake, and prematurity.

  • Vitamin D — baseline 400 IU/day for all breastfed/partially breastfed infants from day one. Scaling: a formula-fed infant taking ≥1 L (≈32 oz)/day of standard fortified formula (≈400 IU/L) already hits 400 IU and needs no drops; below that volume, supplement to close the gap. Stop the separate drop once a weaned toddler drinks ≥1 qt/day of fortified whole milk.
  • Iron — baseline ~1 mg/kg/day from 4 months in exclusively breastfed term infants. Depends on: formula-fed → 0 (iron-fortified formula at ~12 mg/L covers it); preterm/low-birth-weight → ~2 mg/kg/day from ~1 month (lower iron stores at birth); once eating ~2 servings/day of iron-rich/fortified foods, taper the supplement. Worked example: a 7-kg breastfed 5-month-old → ~7 mg/day until solids supply it.
  • Hard zeros (no scaling): no honey before 12 months; no added sugar in early childhood (2025–2030 DGA: avoid until ~age 10); no cow's milk as the main drink before 12 months.

Childhood/toddler — responsive feeding, variety, and a hard sugar ceiling (strong on sugar; moderate on feeding style). Federal guidance tightened in January 2026: the 2025–2030 Dietary Guidelines now advise avoiding added sugars until about age 10 (up from the 2020–2025 "none before age 2"), with no single meal exceeding ~10 g of added sugar USDA/HHS, Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2025–2030; The Nutrition Source, DGA 2025–2030 analysis. As a useful older expert ceiling for school-age children, the AHA's 2017 scientific statement recommends ≤25 g (≈6 teaspoons, ~100 kcal) of added sugar per day for ages 2–18, none before age 2, and sugar-sweetened beverages limited to ≤1 eight-ounce serving per week (the SSB cap is the statement's only Level-of-Evidence-A recommendation; the 25 g ceiling is the weaker Class IIa/Level C) Vos et al., AHA Scientific Statement, Circulation 2017.

Adjust it — the sugar ceiling is an absolute cap, not a percentage, so it does not scale up with appetite. Current federal target: avoid added sugars entirely until ~age 10 (2025–2030 DGA). The AHA-2017 benchmark of ≤25 g/day for ages 2–18 (and 0 g under age 2) remains a useful older expert ceiling once a school-age child does consume added sugar. For a smaller or younger child the cap is, if anything, tighter relative to total intake: 25 g is ~10% of a 1,000-kcal toddler day but only ~5% of a 2,000-kcal teen day, so use the lower-density end (aim well under 25 g) for the youngest in the 2–18 band. One 12-oz soda (~39 g) or one 8-oz juice box (~20–25 g) can blow the entire daily budget; a single sweetened drink also breaches the weekly SSB cap. This ceiling is separate from total carbohydrate — it applies only to added sugar (whole fruit and plain dairy don't count).

Responsive feeding — the caregiver decides what and when, the child decides whether and how much — is the consensus approach to building variety and self-regulation, and picky eating is usually a developmental phase managed by repeated low-pressure exposure, not coercion. The flip side is real: overt restriction, pressure, and "diet talk" around children are associated with later disordered eating and worse self-regulation, so a healthy childhood food environment is as much about how food is offered as what is offered. Whole cow's milk is generally introduced at 12 months; portion-appropriate dairy or fortified alternatives help meet calcium and vitamin D.

Adolescence — growth, iron, and a narrow window to bank bone (strong). Iron needs jump with the growth spurt and menstruation: the RDA is 15 mg/day for girls 14–18 versus 11 mg for boys, and iron deficiency is among the most common nutrient gaps in menstruating teens NIH ODS, Iron. Calcium peaks at 1,300 mg/day (ages 9–18) with 600 IU vitamin D, because adolescence is when most peak bone mass is laid down — a deficit here is hard to recover later NIH ODS, Calcium; NIH ODS, Vitamin D. Athletes need more energy and iron, and require vigilance for low energy availability (RED-S). Adolescence is also the peak-onset window for eating disorders, so the same caution against restriction and weight-focused messaging applies with even higher stakes.

Adjust it — teen iron, calcium, and D scale with sex, menstrual status, diet pattern, and training load.

  • Iron — baseline 15 mg/day (girls 14–18), 11 mg/day (boys 14–18); 8 mg/day ages 9–13. Depends on: vegetarian/vegan → ×1.8 (~27 mg/day for a teen girl), because non-heme iron is ~2–3× less bioavailable; heavy menstrual bleeding or diagnosed deficiency → treat per labs (often 30–60+ mg elemental, clinician-directed), not just the RDA; endurance/high-volume athletes → screen ferritin (foot-strike hemolysis and hepcidin effects raise needs).
  • Calcium — baseline 1,300 mg/day (ages 9–18), the peak of any age group. Depends on: dairy-free/vegan → build from fortified soy milk (~300 mg/cup), tofu set with calcium, fortified juice, greens; amenorrheic athlete (low estrogen) → calcium + D become more urgent for bone, alongside fixing energy availability. There is no upside to exceeding the 3,000 mg/day UL (ages 9–18).
  • Vitamin D — baseline 600 IU/day (15 mcg). Depends on: little sun exposure, darker skin, or higher latitude → the 600 IU RDA is a floor; check 25(OH)D and supplement to keep it ≥20 ng/mL; UL is 4,000 IU/day (ages 9+). Calcium is only useful for bone if D status is adequate.

Pregnancy/lactation — modest extra energy but sharply higher micronutrient needs (strong). Energy rises only +0 kcal in the first trimester, ~+340 kcal/day in the second, and ~+452 in the third (2005 DRI Estimated Energy Requirement) — pregnancy is emphatically not "eating for two" NIH ODS, Pregnancy. Lactation's energy increment is ~+330 kcal/day for the first 6 months and ~+400 for months 7–12 (the true energy cost of exclusive milk production is ~500 kcal/day, with ~170 expected to come from mobilizing pregnancy fat stores). Micronutrient targets climb well above non-pregnant RDAs: 600 mcg DFE folate (with 400–800 mcg supplemental folic acid started ≥1 month before conception to prevent neural-tube defects), 27 mg iron, 220 mcg iodine (290 in lactation), 450 mg choline (550 in lactation), and ≥200 mg DHA on top of a general ≥250 mg/day EPA+DHA baseline NIH ODS, Pregnancy; CDC, Folic Acid. Gestational weight-gain targets follow the IOM 2009 ranges by pre-pregnancy BMI: 28–40 lb if underweight (BMI <18.5), 25–35 lb normal (18.5–24.9), 15–25 lb overweight (25–29.9), 11–20 lb obese (≥30) IOM/NASEM 2009, summary.

Adjust it — pregnancy targets scale by trimester, pre-pregnancy BMI, parity, and diet pattern.

  • Energy — baseline +0 / +340 / +452 kcal by trimester. Depends on: overweight/obese pre-pregnancy → use the low end or less (IOM 2009 notes energy needs are lower; aim for the bottom of the gain range, not added calories); twins → roughly +1 extra serving's worth again and a higher gain target (37–54 lb normal-BMI twins). Don't front-load: there is no increase in the first trimester.
  • Weight gain — baseline by BMI band above, ~0.5–1 lb/week in trimesters 2–3. Worked example: a normal-BMI person (target 25–35 lb) gains ~3–4 lb in T1, then ~1 lb/week. Underweight → up to 40 lb; obese → as low as 11 lb. This is the single clearest "what it depends on" lever in the section.
  • Iron — baseline 27 mg/day. Depends on: vegetarian/vegan → ×1.8 bioavailability adjustment (pair with vitamin C, separate from tea/coffee/calcium); iron-deficiency anemia (Hb <11 g/dL T1/T3) → higher therapeutic dose per labs; routine high-dose iron is not for everyone (constipation, and excess is not benign).
  • Folic acid — baseline 400 mcg supplemental from preconception. Depends on: prior NTD-affected pregnancy → 4,000 mcg (4 mg)/day under clinician supervision (10× the routine dose); certain anti-seizure meds or malabsorption also raise the target.
  • DHA — baseline ≥200 mg/day added. Depends on: little/no fish intake or vegan → algal-oil supplement (the limiting nutrient when the 8–12 oz/week fish window isn't used).

Food-safety avoidances are non-negotiable: listeria-risk foods (unpasteurized dairy, deli meats, refrigerated smoked seafood), high-mercury fish (the FDA "avoid" list: shark, swordfish, king mackerel, tilefish from the Gulf of Mexico, plus bigeye tuna, marlin, orange roughy) — while still eating 8–12 oz/week of lower-mercury fish (≈2–3 servings of ~4 oz; see §19) for the DHA — plus raw/undercooked animal foods and alcohol (no known safe amount) FDA/EPA, Advice About Eating Fish; FDA, Listeria — Food Safety for Moms-to-Be.

Healthy aging — more protein, key micronutrient gaps, and pattern over pill (strong on protein/B12; moderate on longevity diets).
