Older adults resist muscle-building stimuli ("anabolic resistance"), so expert groups recommend 1.0–1.2 g/kg/day protein for healthy elders (more with illness), paired with resistance exercise, to counter sarcopenia Bauer/PROT-AGE, JAMDA 2013 (industry-funded: the EUGMS study group received a grant from Nestlé Nutrition, and most authors disclosed honoraria/grants from medical-nutrition companies — discount the upper end accordingly).

Adjust it — older-adult protein scales with body weight, per-meal distribution, illness, and kidney function.

  • Baseline 1.0–1.2 g/kg/day (vs the 0.8 g/kg adult RDA; canonical protein math in §02/§20). Per-meal: aim ~0.4 g/kg/meal ≈ 25–30 g across 3 meals to clear the higher leucine threshold (~2.5–3 g leucine) that anabolic resistance imposes — front-loading dinner while skimping at breakfast wastes the day's protein.
  • Depends on: acute or chronic illness / hospitalization → 1.2–1.5 g/kg/day; frailty or sarcopenia recovery → up to ~1.5 g/kg/day with resistance training; CKD G3–G5 not on dialysis → 0.55–0.60 g/kg/day (0.6–0.8 if diabetic), clinician-supervised — a restriction that overrides the geriatric push, so this is the key conflict, decided on eGFR (KDOQI 2020; disease targets in §09); on dialysis → back up to ~1.0–1.2 g/kg/day. Worked example: a healthy 70-kg elder → ~70–84 g/day, ~25–30 g per meal; the same person hospitalized with pneumonia → ~84–105 g/day.

B12 absorption falls with age-related atrophic gastritis and PPI/metformin use — deficiency reached ~38% with atrophic gastritis (vs 21% in PPI users, 15% in controls) in one cohort — so adults over ~50 should get B12 from fortified foods or supplements (the crystalline form doesn't need stomach acid) Porter et al., AJCN 2021. Vitamin D, hydration (thirst blunts with age), and attention to appetite and dentition round out the priorities.

Adjust it — B12, vitamin D, and fluids scale with absorption, status, and body size.

  • B12 — baseline 2.4 mcg/day RDA, but met from fortified food/supplements (not just meat) after ~50. Depends on: PPI or metformin use, or atrophic gastritis → check serum B12/methylmalonic acid; oral 100–250 mcg/day or 1,000 mcg if low (crystalline B12 bypasses the acid requirement); strict vegan → supplement is mandatory at any age (§12).
  • Vitamin D — baseline 600 IU/day to age 70, then 800 IU/day (20 mcg) at 71+. Depends on: housebound/institutionalized, darker skin, or measured 25(OH)D <20 ng/mL → 800–2,000 IU/day to correct (UL 4,000 IU/day); pair with calcium 1,000–1,200 mg/day for bone.
  • Hydration — baseline ~1.6 L/day (women) / 2.0 L/day (men) total water from drinks, scaling to ~30 mL/kg/day. Depends on: fever, heat, or diuretics → add ~0.5 L per °C of fever / per hot day; heart failure or hyponatremia → fluid-restricted per clinician (the one direction where less is the target). Blunted thirst means schedule fluids rather than wait for thirst.

For longevity, the strongest human signal is dietary pattern — Mediterranean and Blue Zones eating (largely plant-forward, legume-rich, moderate; §06) — not a supplement. Caloric restriction extends lifespan robustly in animals; the human evidence is short-term and modest: CALERIE 2 (220 randomized, 218 analyzed, 2 years) achieved only ~11.9% restriction — well below its 25% target, since sustained restriction is hard to adhere to — and improved cardiometabolic and some aging biomarkers, but it tested healthy non-obese adults briefly and does not establish a lifespan benefit Dorling et al./CALERIE phase 2, Nutrition Reviews 2020.

What it depends on

  • Exact life stage and sex. Iron jumps from 8 mg (9–13) to 15 mg (girls 14–18) and 27 mg (pregnancy); protein rises from 0.8 to 1.0–1.2 g/kg after ~65; allergen-introduction risk stratifies by eczema/egg-allergy status (high-risk → IgE/skin test before peanut).
  • Feeding mode in infancy. Breastfed infants need 400 IU vitamin D from day one and ~1 mg/kg/day iron from 4 months; formula at ≥1 L/day (≈400 IU + ~12 mg iron/L) covers both.
  • Pre-pregnancy BMI. Sets the gestational weight-gain range (IOM 2009): 28–40 / 25–35 / 15–25 / 11–20 lb for underweight/normal/overweight/obese.
  • Diet pattern. Vegan/vegetarian raises non-heme iron needs ×1.8, makes B12 supplementation mandatory, and shifts DHA to algal oil and iodine to deliberate sources (§12, §17).
  • Medications and gut health. PPIs, metformin, and atrophic gastritis cut B12 absorption — deficiency ~38% with atrophic gastritis vs 15% controls — so use crystalline B12 (§15).

Practical takeaways

  • Infants: exclusive milk to ~6 months, then iron-rich complementary foods; introduce peanut/egg early (risk-stratify high-risk babies with a clinician); no honey before 12 months; avoid added sugar in early childhood (2025–2030 DGA: until ~age 10); give breastfed babies 400 IU vitamin D daily (skip if formula-fed ≥1 L/day) and ~1 mg/kg/day iron from 4 months (skip if formula-fed; ~2 mg/kg if preterm).
  • Toddlers/children: offer variety with low-pressure repeated exposure; the 2025–2030 DGA now advises avoiding added sugar until ~age 10, with the older AHA-2017 ceiling of ≤25 g/day (none before 2) as a fallback benchmark for school-age kids; keep SSBs to ≤1 eight-oz serving/week; the cap is absolute, so go lower for the youngest; avoid restriction, weight comments, and "good/bad food" framing.
  • Teens: prioritize iron (menstruating girls 15 mg/day, boys 11 mg, ×1.8 if vegetarian) and calcium (1,300 mg) + vitamin D (600 IU, more if status is low) for peak bone mass; watch athletes for low energy availability and watch everyone for disordered eating.
  • Pregnancy: 400–800 mcg folic acid from ≥1 month before conception (4 mg if prior NTD), then 600 mcg folate, 27 mg iron (×1.8 vegetarian), 220 mcg iodine, 450 mg choline, ≥200 mg added DHA; add +0/+340/+452 kcal by trimester (low end if overweight/obese); follow IOM weight-gain ranges by pre-pregnancy BMI; avoid listeria foods, high-mercury fish, raw animal foods, and all alcohol. Lactation: ~+330–400 kcal/day, iodine 290 mcg, choline 550 mg.
  • Older adults: raise protein to 1.0–1.2 g/kg/day (~25–30 g per meal), 1.2–1.5 with illness, but 0.55–0.8 g/kg if CKD pre-dialysis, plus resistance training; supplement or fortify B12 and check vitamin D (800 IU at 71+); mind hydration (~30 mL/kg/day), appetite, and dental health; favor a Mediterranean/Blue Zones pattern over longevity supplements.

Unsettled or debated

  • The optimal protein target for older and sarcopenic adults is debated (estimates ~1.0 to ~1.5+ g/kg/day); the direction (above the 0.8 g/kg RDA) is solid consensus, the exact number is not.
  • Caloric restriction for human longevity is genuinely unsettled — compelling in animals, but human data (CALERIE) are short-term and show biomarker shifts, not proven lifespan extension; sustained restriction also risks muscle and bone loss, especially in older adults.
  • General-population early allergen introduction beyond peanut/egg (e.g., the full EAT panel) has weaker trial support; the strongest evidence remains early peanut in high-risk infants.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

17. Special Diets and Restrictions: Vegetarian/Vegan, Gluten-Free, and Food Allergies

Bottom line: Any restriction is only as healthy as the planning behind it — a planned vegan diet is excellent (B12 is non-negotiable), celiac/allergy exclusions are absolute, but never self-impose a restriction without a diagnosis, because every food group you cut is nutrition you may not replace.

Some diets are chosen; others are imposed by biology. A vegetarian eats by conviction; a person with celiac disease or a peanut allergy has no choice at all. The two get lumped together as "special diets," but they sit at opposite ends of a spectrum, and the most important rule cuts across both: restriction is only as healthy as the planning behind it. A well-built plant-based diet is one of the healthiest patterns we know of; a poorly built one — or a needlessly restrictive one adopted on a hunch — narrows nutrition for no benefit.

The single biggest mistake in this whole territory is self-imposing a restriction without a diagnosis. Cutting out gluten, dairy, or whole food groups because they feel "inflammatory" usually does nothing but shrink the variety that protects you, raise your grocery bill, and (in the gluten case) sabotage the test that could have told you the truth. Diagnose first, restrict second.

The other recurring theme is that almost any restriction can be made healthful with attention. Vegan, gluten-free for celiac disease, allergen-avoidant, halal, kosher, Ramadan-fasting — none of these is inherently deficient. What matters is whether the gaps each one creates get filled.

Key evidence

Vegetarian and vegan diets are nutritionally adequate for all life stages — when planned (strong, with caveats). The Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics' position is unambiguous: "appropriately planned vegetarian, including vegan, diets are healthful, nutritionally adequate, and may provide health benefits for the prevention and treatment of certain diseases," and are appropriate "for all stages of the life cycle, including pregnancy, lactation, infancy, childhood, adolescence, older adulthood, and for athletes" Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics 2016. The benefits are real and measured. A meta-analysis of 30 randomized trials found vegetarian and vegan diets lowered total cholesterol, LDL cholesterol, and apoB versus omnivorous diets Koch et al., Eur Heart J 2023, and Stanford's identical-twin trial saw LDL fall further on a vegan diet (from ~110.7 to 95.5 mg/dL) alongside lower insulin and body weight Stanford Medicine 2023. Vegetarians and vegans also tend to have lower BMI, blood pressure, and rates of type 2 diabetes and ischemic heart disease.

But "appropriately planned" is load-bearing. The non-negotiable gap is vitamin B12: there is no reliable plant source, deficiency causes irreversible nerve damage and anemia, and every vegan (and most vegetarians) must use a supplement or reliably fortified foods — this is not optional NIH ODS, B12 (see §12).

Adjust it (B12 dose). The RDA is only 2.4 mcg/day, but you cannot dose a supplement at the RDA, because intrinsic-factor–mediated absorption saturates at ~1.5–2 mcg per dose and the passive route that handles the rest absorbs only ~1% — at a single 500 mcg tablet about 2% is absorbed, and at 1,000 mcg about 1.3% NIH ODS, B12. Two equivalent regimens cover the gap: (a) a daily cyanocobalamin dose of 50–100 mcg (frequent small doses use the saturable, efficient route), or (b) an infrequent large dose of 1,000 mcg taken 2–3×/week (relies on the ~1% passive route — 1,000 mcg × ~1.3% ≈ 13 mcg absorbed per dose, ample). Scale it to your situation: fortified foods reliably eaten (e.g., 2–3 servings/day of fortified plant milk or nutritional yeast at ~1–2.5 mcg/serving) can substitute for a low-dose supplement but rarely replace it entirely; age >50 or low stomach acid / metformin / PPIs → favor the supplement over food-bound B12 and stay at the high end; established deficiency → clinician-directed 1,000 mcg/day (or intramuscular) until repleted, then maintenance; pregnancy/lactation → don't drop below daily 50 mcg, since fetal/infant stores depend on maternal intake. (See §12.) The other nutrients to actively manage:

  • Iron — plant (non-heme) iron is far less bioavailable; the NIH sets the iron requirement 1.8× higher for vegetarians, and pairing iron-rich foods with vitamin C meaningfully boosts absorption while tea/coffee tannins blunt it NIH ODS, Iron.
  • Adjust it (iron target). Baseline RDA × 1.8 for any vegetarian/vegan: men 19+ and women 51+ 8 mg → ~14 mg/day; menstruating women 19–50 18 mg → ~32 mg/day; pregnancy 27 mg → ~49 mg/day. Modifiers with numbers: pair each iron-rich meal with ~50–100 mg vitamin C (e.g., one orange or 1/2 cup bell pepper) to multiply non-heme absorption roughly 2–3×; separate tea/coffee by ≥1 hour (tannins cut absorption up to ~60%); heavy menstrual loss or diagnosed deficiency → clinician may add ~30–65 mg elemental iron as a supplement (don't self-prescribe high doses — excess iron is harmful). Confirm status with ferritin rather than guessing.
  • Zinc — also less bioavailable from plants (phytates bind it); favor legumes, seeds, and whole grains, and soak/sprout/ferment them to cut phytate Hunt, AJCN 2003.
  • Adjust it (zinc target). The IOM judges that zinc requirements may be up to 50% greater for vegetarians whose staples are grains and legumes (dietary phytate:zinc molar ratio >15:1) IOM DRI, Zinc. So scale the RDA by up to 1.5×: men 11 mg → ~16.5 mg/day; women 8 mg → ~12 mg/day; pregnancy 11 mg → ~16.5 mg/day; lactation 12 mg → ~18 mg/day. The multiplier shrinks toward 1.0× as you lower the phytate:zinc ratio — soaking/sprouting legumes and grains and leavening bread (yeast fermentation) can roughly halve phytate, so a mixed plant diet with such prep sits nearer 1.2–1.3×.
  • Calcium, vitamin D, iodine — at risk on vegan diets; use fortified plant milks, consider supplements, and don't assume non-iodized salt covers iodine NIH ODS, Iodine.
  • Adjust it (calcium). Baseline RDA 1,000 mg/day for adults 19–50 and men 51–70; 1,200 mg/day for women 51+ and everyone 71+ NIH ODS, Calcium. Practical fill: most calcium-fortified plant milks supply ~300 mg per cup (check the label — ranges ~120–450 mg), so 2–3 cups/day plus calcium-set tofu (~200–400 mg/100 g), fortified juice, and low-oxalate greens (kale, bok choy) usually reaches target without pills. Modifiers: pregnancy/lactation stays at the age-band RDA (no increase 19–50); postmenopausal or low bone density → hold the full 1,200 mg and pair with adequate D; keep total intake <2,000–2,500 mg/day (the UL).
  • Adjust it (vitamin D). RDA 15 mcg (600 IU)/day ages 1–70, 20 mcg (800 IU)/day if >70, targeting serum 25(OH)D ≥50 nmol/L (≥20 ng/mL) NIH ODS, Vitamin D. This is not vegan-specific — it scales with sun exposure and skin tone, not diet: little midday sun, higher latitude (>~37°), darker skin, obesity, or winter → supplement at the RDA or higher and verify by blood test; reliable regular sun on bare skin may cover it. Vegans should choose vitamin D3 from lichen (most D3 is animal-derived) or D2. Don't exceed the UL of 100 mcg (4,000 IU)/day without a measured deficiency and clinician guidance.
  • Adjust it (iodine). RDA 150 mcg/day for adults, 220 mcg/day in pregnancy, 290 mcg/day in lactation NIH ODS, Iodine. Vegan diets are a notable gap because dairy and fish are major iodine sources. Fill from iodized salt (~45 mcg iodine per gram of salt, so a 1/4 tsp ≈ ~70 mcg — about half a day's need) or a supplement; do not rely on sea salt, pink/Himalayan salt, or unspecified plant milks, which are unreliable. Caution the other way: kelp/seaweed can deliver thousands of mcg and push you past the UL of 1,100 mcg/day, so treat it as a supplement, not a free seasoning. Pregnancy/lactation: a supplement providing ~150 mcg is widely advised given the higher targets.
  • Omega-3 (EPA/DHA) — plant ALA converts poorly (only a few percent of ALA becomes EPA and <1% becomes DHA); an algal DHA/EPA supplement is the clean fix, and is specifically advised in pregnancy/lactation for those who don't eat fish (see §12).
  • Adjust it (algal omega-3). Baseline ~250 mg/day combined EPA+DHA for general adults (the common cardiovascular intake target), best met with ~200–300 mg of preformed algal DHA since plant ALA won't reliably top it up. Modifiers with numbers: pregnancy/lactation → add ~100–200 mg DHA/day on top, i.e., aim ~300 mg DHA total (ISSFAL); high triglycerides or clinician-directed cardiac use → much higher pharmacologic doses (1–4 g) under supervision. Keep total EPA+DHA ≤3 g/day, with ≤2 g/day from supplements (FDA). Background ALA still matters: ~1.1 g/day (women) to 1.6 g/day (men) from flax, chia, walnuts, or canola supports the small endogenous conversion.
  • Protein — adequate but requires variety across legumes, soy, grains, nuts, and seeds to cover all essential amino acids over the day.
  • Adjust it (plant protein). Use the canonical targets from §02/§20 — RDA 0.8 g/kg/day, with ~1.2–1.6 g/kg for active people and older adults — but because plant protein is somewhat less digestible (lower DIAAS) and lower in lysine, a practical rule is to aim toward the upper end (+~10%) of whatever band §02 gives you, and to anchor lysine with legumes/soy at most meals (soy and lentils are lysine-rich; grains and nuts are not). Numeric handles: ~0.4 g protein/kg per meal across 3–4 meals to maximize muscle protein synthesis; age >65 → ~1.2–1.5 g/kg/day to counter anabolic resistance; endurance/strength training → 1.4–2.0 g/kg/day; CKD without dialysis → lower, ~0.6–0.8 g/kg per §09, not higher. "Complementary proteins" need only balance over the whole day, not the same meal.

Extra care applies to infants, children, and pregnancy, where the margin for error is thinner — these groups should be planned with a clinician or dietitian rather than improvised.

Gluten-free is a medical treatment, not a wellness diet (strong). A strict gluten-free diet is the only treatment for celiac disease (~1% of the population), an autoimmune condition where gluten damages the small intestine; it is also required for wheat allergy and helps a subset with non-celiac gluten sensitivity AAFP/ACG 2024. For everyone else, the evidence does not support a benefit: a review of gluten avoidance in non-celiac patients concluded that "no nutritional advantage was found for gluten-free foods" and that most people on a gluten-free diet "do not have a physiologic requirement for the diet and likely do not derive substantial benefit" Niland & Cash, Gastroenterol Hepatol 2018. Commercial gluten-free products are frequently lower in fiber and protein and higher in fat, salt, and (in some categories) sugar than their wheat counterparts Fry et al., J Hum Nutr Diet 2018 — and cost markedly more (averaging ~159% more in the UK, and ranging from 46% to 443% more across categories on the Norwegian market) Myhrstad et al., Food Nutr Res 2021. The critical procedural point: get tested while still eating gluten. Celiac serology (tissue transglutaminase IgA) requires active gluten consumption to be accurate; remove gluten first and you may force an unnecessary, uncomfortable "gluten challenge" later to confirm the diagnosis ACG 2024. (For gut symptoms without celiac disease, see the low-FODMAP approach in §09.)

Adjust it (how strict, and for whom). Strictness scales with the diagnosis, not preference:

  • Confirmed celiac disease → lifelong, near-zero gluten. The dose-response evidence puts the daily threshold for mucosal harm between 10 and 50 mg/day: 50 mg/day for 3 months damages the intestine in most patients, while 10 mg/day does not Catassi et al., AJCN 2007. Practically that means "gluten-free"–labeled foods (<20 ppm gluten, the FDA/Codex limit) FDA gluten-free rule, plus managing cross-contact (separate toaster, shared-fryer awareness) — a few slices of regular bread (~2–4 g gluten) is orders of magnitude over the limit.
  • Wheat allergy → avoid wheat protein specifically (IgE-mediated), but other gluten grains (barley, rye) may be fine — this is a different restriction than celiac.
  • Non-celiac gluten sensitivity → no validated threshold; many tolerate small amounts, and the trigger is often FODMAPs rather than gluten (see "Unsettled," below). Trial a structured reduction rather than zero-tolerance.
  • Pre-test (any of the above suspected) → keep eating gluten until testing is done. If you've already gone gluten-free, a formal gluten challenge means resuming ≥3 g gluten/day (≈1.5–2 slices of wheat bread) for at least 6–8 weeks (guidelines span 6–12) before serology/biopsy so the test isn't falsely negative — which is exactly why removing gluten before testing is the costly mistake ACG 2024.
  • No diagnosis → there is no threshold to manage, because there is no benefit to manage it for.

Food allergies: immune, potentially life-threatening, and distinct from intolerance (strong). A true food allergy is an IgE-mediated immune response — reacting within minutes, capable of anaphylaxis — and affects roughly 1 in 10 U.S. adults and ~1 in 12 children by population survey Gupta et al., epidemiology review, PMC 2021. This is fundamentally different from an intolerance (e.g., lactose intolerance, an enzyme shortfall affecting a large share of adults), which causes discomfort but is not immune-mediated and is not dangerous. The U.S. recognizes nine major allergens requiring labeling: milk, egg, fish, crustacean shellfish, tree nuts, peanuts, wheat, soybeans, and — since the FASTER Act took effect January 2023 — sesame FDA 2023. Management rests on label-reading, avoiding cross-contamination, and carrying epinephrine as the first-line treatment for anaphylaxis (antihistamines do not treat it). Two developments are reshaping the field: early introduction prevents allergy — the LEAP trial cut peanut allergy by 81% (overall intention-to-treat: 17.2% with avoidance vs 3.2% with consumption; 13.7% → 1.9% in the larger skin-prick-negative stratum) by feeding peanut to high-risk infants enrolled at 4–11 months rather than avoiding it Du Toit et al., NEJM 2015 (funded by NIAID/NIH, with partial support from the National Peanut Board) (see §16) — and oral immunotherapy now exists: Palforzia, FDA-approved in 2020 for peanut-allergic children, desensitizes but does not cure, so epinephrine is still required PALISADE, NEJM 2018 (industry-funded: Aimmune Therapeutics, the manufacturer).

Adjust it (epinephrine and avoidance). Avoidance of a true IgE-mediated allergen is absolute — there is no safe dose to titrate, so this is the one "recommendation" with no scaling: zero exposure. What you do scale is the rescue plan. Carry two epinephrine auto-injectors, not one, because ~10–20% of anaphylaxis episodes need a second dose; give the second 5–15 minutes after the first if symptoms don't improve or worsen. Dose follows weight (the prescribed device, not you, fixes this): an 0.1 mg infant device exists for 7.5–15 kg, 0.15 mg ("Jr") for roughly 7.5/15–25 kg, 0.3 mg for ≥25–30 kg, and 0.5 mg for larger adults — your allergist sets the right one. After any epinephrine use, seek emergency care (biphasic reactions can recur hours later). Antihistamines and inhalers are adjuncts only — they do not substitute for epinephrine.

Religious and ethical patterns can all be healthy (moderate). Halal, kosher, Buddhist/Hindu and Jain vegetarianism, and the fasting of Ramadan (and other traditions) impose structure rather than deficiency. Each can be built to meet needs — plant-based religious vegetarianism needs the same B12/iron/omega-3 attention as any vegan diet, and Ramadan benefits from hydration and balanced pre-dawn/post-sunset meals (intermittent fasting is covered in §11). Structure is not the same as inadequacy.

Adjust it (Ramadan). Hit your full day's calorie and protein targets (§20/§02) inside the eating window — don't under-eat. Spread fluid intake to ~2–3 L across iftar to suhoor (more in hot climates or with heavy sweating; see the hydration math in §07); make suhoor a low-glycemic, protein-containing meal (e.g., ~25–40 g protein plus slow carbs and fat) to blunt daytime hunger, and break the fast at iftar with water and a moderate first course rather than a single large load. Modifiers: diabetes on insulin/sulfonylureas, pregnancy, or chronic illness → individualized medical clearance and dose adjustment before fasting, as hypoglycemia and dehydration risk rise.

What it depends on

  • Diagnosis vs. preference. A medically required restriction (celiac, IgE allergy) is non-negotiable and precise. A chosen restriction (vegan, religious) is a design problem — solvable with planning.
  • How strict. Vegan demands more supplementation (B12 always, often D/iodine/omega-3) than lacto-ovo vegetarian, which may still get B12 and calcium from dairy/eggs. Celiac demands near-zero gluten — staying under the <20 ppm "gluten-free" label limit and below the ~10–50 mg/day harm threshold, trace cross-contamination included — whereas non-celiac gluten sensitivity often tolerates small amounts and lacks any validated threshold.
  • Life stage. Infants, children, and pregnant/lactating people have the least margin; plant-based or allergy-restricted diets in these groups warrant professional oversight (§16).
  • Whether the gaps get filled. The diet's healthiness is decided less by what's removed than by what replaces it — whole foods and targeted supplements, not ultra-processed "free-from" products.

Practical takeaways

  • Going vegan? A numeric daily checklist (scale per the "Adjust it" notes above): B12 50–100 mcg/day or 1,000 mcg 2–3×/week (no exceptions); iron ~1.8× RDA (≈14 mg men/older women, ≈32 mg menstruating women) paired with vitamin C; zinc up to 1.5× RDA (≈12–16.5 mg) with soaked/sprouted legumes; iodine 150 mcg (iodized salt or supplement, not sea salt); calcium 1,000–1,200 mg (≈3 cups fortified plant milk + tofu); vitamin D 600–800 IU as needed by sun/latitude; algal DHA/EPA ~200–300 mg; and varied protein at the upper end of your §02 band. Plan pregnancy and childhood diets with a dietitian.
  • Suspect celiac or gluten issues? Test first — while still eating gluten. Don't go gluten-free as a diet trend; for non-celiac eaters it adds cost and often subtracts fiber.
  • Choose whole foods over "free-from" packaged products, which tend to be lower-fiber and higher in sugar/fat/salt across both gluten-free and vegan aisles.
  • Food allergy: know your nine major allergens, read labels every time, carry two epinephrine auto-injectors, and treat anaphylaxis with epinephrine first. Ask an allergist about oral immunotherapy.
  • For infants, introduce allergenic foods early (including peanut and egg) rather than delaying — around 6 months, when the infant is developmentally ready for solids. Adjust it by risk tier (NIAID 2017): low risk / mild–moderate eczema → introduce peanut and other allergens around 6 months at home, no testing needed; high risk (severe eczema and/or egg allergy) → see a clinician for peanut-specific IgE or skin-prick testing first, then introduce peanut as early as 4–6 months if testing allows. Once started, the LEAP-style maintenance "dose" is ~2 g peanut protein per feeding ≈ ~6 g/week spread over ≥3 meals/week (about 2 tsp of smooth peanut butter thinned for safety — never whole nuts or globs, a choking hazard), continued regularly through age ~5 years. This prevents allergy (§16).
  • Don't self-impose restrictions without a diagnosis. Every food group you cut is nutrition (and pleasure) you may not get back. See §03 (micronutrients), §08 (personalization), and §12 (supplements).

Unsettled or debated

Non-celiac gluten sensitivity (NCGS) is the genuine gray zone. It is a real clinical entity for some — symptoms improve off gluten with celiac disease and wheat allergy excluded — but blinded re-challenge studies suggest that in many people the trigger is fermentable carbohydrate (FODMAPs), not gluten itself, and a nocebo effect is common. There is no validated biomarker, so diagnosis is one of exclusion (§09, §15). The pragmatic stance: rule out celiac disease properly first, then trial a structured low-FODMAP or gluten reduction with professional guidance rather than indefinite self-restriction.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

18. Diet and the Brain: Mental Health and Cognition

Bottom line: The same Mediterranean-style pattern that wins everywhere helps mood as a real adjunct to professional care (not a treatment), while the brain-protection claim is promising-but-unproven — so eat the ordinary good diet, no special 'brain diet' needed.

The brain is a hungry, fatty, glucose-thirsty organ, and the idea that what you eat changes how you feel and think has obvious appeal. The evidence is real, but it is also smaller and more uneven than the wellness internet suggests. For mental health — specifically depression — there is now genuine randomized-trial evidence that improving diet quality can help, on top of usual care. For cognition and dementia, the observational signals are strong and consistent, yet the one well-powered randomized trial of a "brain diet" came up empty. Holding both of those facts at once is the honest position.

A safety caveat belongs at the very top, not the bottom. Diet is a supportive, adjunct factor — never a treatment for clinical depression, anxiety, or dementia. The depression trials below were run alongside the medications and therapy people were already receiving; not instead of them. If you or someone you know is struggling, the right move is professional care — a doctor, therapist, or crisis line — and eating better is something you can do in addition, not a reason to delay or replace treatment. The mechanisms that plausibly link diet to mood — inflammation, the gut–brain axis (§15), vascular health, and blood-glucose stability — are the same ones that make a generally healthy diet worth eating regardless of its effect on your brain.

The reassuring throughline: the pattern that shows up in the mental-health and cognition literature is the same Mediterranean-style, minimally-processed, plant-forward pattern that wins everywhere else in this guide (§06). You do not need a special "brain diet." You need the ordinary good one.

Key evidence

Diet can improve depression — the SMILES trial (moderate; first of its kind). SMILES (Jacka et al., 2017) was the first randomized controlled trial to test diet as an adjunctive treatment for moderate-to-severe major depression. Sixty-seven adults, most already on medication or in therapy, were randomized to seven sessions of dietetic support toward a modified Mediterranean diet, or to a matched "befriending" social-support control. At 12 weeks the diet group improved by about 7.1 more points on the MADRS depression scale (Cohen's d = −1.16, a large effect), and reached remission at 32.3% versus 8.0% in controls — a number-needed-to-treat of roughly 4 Jacka et al., BMC Medicine 2017 (government-funded by the NHMRC, with in-kind industry support including Meat and Livestock Australia, which funded the biochemistry analyses). The result is striking but must be read with caution: it is a single small trial (only 56 completers), it could not blind participants to whether they were getting cooking advice or chit-chat, and improvements tracked with how much diet quality actually changed.

Adjust it — the realistic, personalized expectation is not "minus 7 MADRS points for everyone." That 7.1-point gap and NNT of ~4 came from people with moderate-to-severe depression eating a genuinely poor baseline diet, almost all already on medication or therapy, who then made a large diet change over 12 weeks. How it scales: the benefit is roughly proportional to how much your diet quality actually improves — SMILES saw mood gains track with diet-score change, so someone already eating a near-Mediterranean pattern has little headroom and should expect little additional mood effect. The sober cross-trial estimate is much smaller: pooling longer (≥3-month) trials gives only a small effect (e.g., SMD ≈ −0.23 for calorie restriction, low certainty; see below). What it depends on, with numbers: poor baseline diet + clinical depression → plan a ~12-week structured change and judge by your own symptom scale, expecting a modest add-on lift; already Mediterranean-ish → treat diet as maintenance, not a mood lever; mild/subclinical low mood → evidence is thinner and the expected effect smaller still; any case → this is an adjunct to professional care, not a substitute (see safety caveat above).

Supporting trials and meta-analyses (moderate). HELFIMED (Parletta et al., 2019) randomized 152 adults with self-reported depression to Mediterranean-diet hampers, cooking workshops, and fish oil versus social groups, and found greater improvement in mental health and quality of life in the diet arm Parletta et al., Nutritional Neuroscience 2019. Pooling the trials, a meta-analysis of 16 RCTs concluded that dietary improvement significantly reduced depressive symptoms — though with no measurable effect on anxiety Firth et al., Psychosomatic Medicine 2019. The most rigorous recent synthesis is more sober: a 2025 review of 25 longer (≥3-month) trials found that calorie restriction and low-fat diets produced only small reductions in depressive symptoms (e.g., SMD −0.23 for calorie restriction) at low certainty, with too little evidence to judge anxiety Abukmail et al., Annals of Internal Medicine 2025. The fair summary: a real but modest adjunct effect, strongest in people whose diets start poor.

Observational links: Western/ultra-processed diets track with more depression (moderate, observational). Across prospective cohorts, higher diet quality predicts lower depression risk. Lassale et al.'s meta-analysis found the strongest signal for the Mediterranean diet — about a 33% lower risk of depression for the highest vs. lowest adherence (RR 0.67, 95% CI 0.55–0.82) — and a parallel benefit for less-inflammatory diets (RR 0.76, 0.63–0.92) Lassale et al., Molecular Psychiatry 2019. At the other end, in the Nurses' Health Study II (31,712 women), the highest ultra-processed-food intake was associated with a 49% higher risk of depression (HR 1.49, 1.26–1.76, strict definition), with artificially sweetened beverages flagged most strongly (HR 1.37) Samuthpongtorn et al., JAMA Network Open 2023. These are correlations — reverse causation (low mood drives comfort-food eating) and shared lifestyle factors are very plausible — so they raise, but cannot prove, causation.

Adjust it — these are between-groups contrasts, so your personal "dose" is how far you move along the pattern. The 33% lower depression risk (RR 0.67) is highest vs. lowest Mediterranean adherence, and the 49% higher risk (HR 1.49) is the top vs. bottom band of ultra-processed intake (roughly >8.8 vs. <4 UPF servings/day in that cohort) — not what one extra salad or one fewer soda does. How it scales: the realistic lever for most people is directional, in steps — each move toward more vegetables/legumes/whole grains/olive oil and away from ultra-processed foods and sugary or artificially-sweetened drinks (the latter flagged at HR 1.37) shifts you along that gradient; the benefit per step is small and unproven causally, so don't expect a 33% personal drop. What it depends on, with numbers: currently near the bottom (mostly ultra-processed) → the largest expected headroom, so target a few concrete swaps (e.g., cut sweetened/diet drinks toward 0/day, add legumes 3–4×/week); already near the top → little room to move and little expected change; outcome → these figures are for depression, not anxiety (where the evidence is thinner) or dementia (different literature, below).

Plausible mechanisms (limited but biologically coherent). Four threads recur: chronic low-grade inflammation (pro-inflammatory diets associate with both depression and elevated CRP/IL-6); the gut–brain axis, where fiber-fed microbes produce short-chain fatty acids and influence neuro-immune signaling (§15); vascular health, since the same diet that protects arteries protects the brain's small vessels; and blood-glucose stability, since the swings driven by refined carbohydrate can affect energy, irritability, and concentration. None of these is a proven causal pathway in humans, but together they make the diet–mood link mechanistically credible.

Cognition and dementia — strong observational signal, null trial (contested). Cohort studies are remarkably consistent: across prospective studies (>34,000 participants), high Mediterranean-diet adherence is associated with roughly 20% lower risk of cognitive disorders (pooled RR ~0.79, 0.70–0.90) and slower decline Wu & Sun, Scientific Reports 2017, and the MIND diet's original cohorts suggested dramatic protection. But the decisive randomized test failed: a rigorous 3-year RCT of the MIND diet found no cognitive benefit versus a control diet (§06) Barnes et al., NEJM 2023. This is the field's cautionary tale — the observational story may reflect confounding, or three years may be too short to move a decades-long process. Honest verdict: promising, not proven.

Adjust it — there is no number to "dose" here for brain protection, so set expectations accordingly. The 20% lower risk (RR 0.79) is an observational, highest-vs-lowest contrast that a 3-year randomized trial could not reproduce (global-cognition difference just +0.035 units, p = 0.23, with both arms losing ~5 kg). What that means for you, with numbers: do not adopt a Mediterranean/MIND pattern for the purpose of preventing dementia expecting a measurable cognitive payoff — the trial evidence for that specific goal is currently null. The defensible reason to eat this way is its proven cardiometabolic and vascular benefit (§06, §09), which protects the brain's small vessels as a by-product; the time horizon for any cognitive effect is decades, not the weeks-to-months over which mood can shift, so judge it on heart/metabolic markers you can actually track, not on a felt cognitive change.

Omega-3 and B-vitamins/homocysteine (mixed). For depression, EPA-predominant omega-3 shows a modest benefit, often as an add-on to antidepressants rather than a standalone fix. The meta-analytic signal points to formulations that are ≥60% EPA at ~720–1,000 mg EPA/day, with no added benefit above ~1 g/day; pure-EPA augmentation of antidepressants gave a small-to-moderate effect (SMD ≈ −0.44), while DHA-predominant formulations showed essentially none Liao et al., Translational Psychiatry 2019. The evidence is heterogeneous, though — the pooled signal is driven by EPA-predominant formulations and by trials where it was added to antidepressants, and several well-powered RCTs were null — so don't overstate it. For cognition, B-vitamin/homocysteine-lowering is genuinely mixed: the VITACOG trial slowed brain atrophy and cognitive decline only in the subgroup with high baseline homocysteine, while a Cochrane review of older adults with mild cognitive impairment found little or no cognitive benefit from B vitamins Cochrane, McCleery et al. 2018. Supplements are not a substitute for the underlying dietary pattern.

Adjust it — omega-3 for low mood is an adjunct, dialed to EPA content, not bottle size. Baseline: ~1 g/day of EPA specifically (not total omega-3, not DHA), from a supplement that is ≥60% EPA, on top of (never instead of) clinician-directed care. How it scales: benefit appears across roughly 720–1,000 mg EPA/day and does not increase beyond ~1 g/day — going to 2–4 g buys side-effects (reflux, loose stools, a small bleeding-risk bump), not mood. Read the label: a "1,000 mg fish-oil softgel" typically delivers only ~300 mg EPA + 200 mg DHA, so hitting 1 g EPA often means 2–3 such softgels or a concentrated EPA product. What it depends on, with numbers: predominant depression with low diet quality → worth a 6–8-week trial at ~1 g EPA/day as an add-on; no depression, general brain "insurance" → skip the pill and get omega-3 from 2 servings/week of oily fish (≈250–500 mg EPA+DHA/day equivalent, the §06 target) instead; on anticoagulants/antiplatelets or pre-surgery → doses ≥3 g/day raise bleeding risk, so stay ≤1 g and clear it with your prescriber; pregnancy → prioritize DHA (≈200–300 mg/day) for fetal needs over EPA-for-mood, and choose low-mercury sources. If 8 weeks at ~1 g EPA/day changes nothing, stop — it is not working for you. (For B-vitamins, the "adjustable" lever is a test, not a dose: only worth considering if a clinician documents elevated homocysteine — see §05/§09.)

Caffeine, alcohol, and blood sugar. Caffeine reliably boosts short-term alertness; moderate coffee intake is modestly associated with lower depression risk in observational data, but that link is correlational — reverse causation (low mood reduces coffee intake) and confounding are plausible, and randomized effects on mood are mixed — and high doses can worsen anxiety and wreck sleep. EFSA judges intakes up to ~400 mg/day of caffeine from all sources, and single doses up to ~200 mg (~3 mg/kg), safe for healthy non-pregnant adults EFSA caffeine opinion 2015. Alcohol is a central-nervous-system depressant that disrupts sleep architecture (notably REM) and is bidirectionally tied to depression and anxiety — it tends to worsen the very moods people drink to relieve MedlinePlus: Alcohol; §07. And large blood-sugar swings from refined carbohydrate can drive transient irritability, fatigue, and poor concentration, which is one practical reason the steadier, fiber-rich Mediterranean pattern feels better day to day.

Adjust it — caffeine: a per-body, per-clock ceiling, not a flat "4 cups." Baseline: ≤400 mg/day total and ≤200 mg in any single dose for a healthy ~70-kg adult. How it scales: the safe single dose is roughly 3 mg/kg, so ~200 mg at 70 kg, ~150 mg at 50 kg, ~270 mg at 90 kg; one brewed 240 mL (8 oz) coffee is ~95 mg, an espresso shot ~65 mg, black tea ~40–50 mg, a 250 mL energy drink ~80 mg, so "4 cups" is a rough stand-in for the 400 mg cap, not the cap itself. Timing for alertness: caffeine's half-life is ~5 hours (range 3–7), so a 200 mg afternoon dose still has ~100 mg active 5 hours later; even ~100 mg near bedtime can lengthen the time to fall asleep and shorten sleep in sensitive people, so set a personal cutoff ~8 hours before bed (e.g., last dose by ~2–3 pm for an 11 pm bedtime). What it depends on, with numbers: pregnant or lactating → ≤200 mg/day; anxiety, panic, or palpitations → cut toward ~100–200 mg/day and watch the single dose; poor sleep → push the cutoff to ≥10 hours pre-bed or drop the afternoon dose entirely; slow metabolizer / caffeine-sensitive (CYP1A2) → treat ~3 mg/kg as a ceiling you rarely approach; adolescents and children → ~3 mg/kg/day is the safe limit (far below 400 mg).

Adjust it — alcohol: there is no "mood-helpful" dose; the only question is how much harm. Baseline for brain/mood purposes: less is better, and zero is a defensible target, since alcohol is a depressant that fragments sleep. How it scales: even 1–2 standard drinks (a U.S. standard drink = 14 g pure alcohol ≈ 355 mL/12 oz beer at 5%, 148 mL/5 oz wine at 12%, or 44 mL/1.5 oz spirits at 40%) within ~3 hours of bed measurably suppresses REM and worsens second-half-of-night sleep, which feeds next-day low mood. What it depends on, with numbers: if you drink, stay at or below the long-standing moderate-use ceiling — ≤2 drinks/day for men, ≤1/day for women (the 2020–2025 U.S. limits; the 2025–2030 update drops the explicit numbers for a blunter "drink less for better health," which only reinforces a lower target — see §07), and don't "save up" doses; active depression/anxiety or any sleep complaint → aim for 0, since it reliably worsens the moods people drink to relieve; on antidepressants, sedatives, or sleep aids → 0 (additive CNS depression); pregnancy → 0. Drinking is never an evidence-based mood treatment — if you are using it to manage feelings, that is a reason to talk to a clinician (see safety caveat above).

What it depends on

  • Your starting diet. The mood benefit is largest when you begin with a poor, ultra-processed diet — there is more room to improve.
  • Whether it is adjunct or substitute. Diet helps alongside therapy and medication. It is not a replacement, and treating it as one is the real risk.
  • Outcome. Depression has supportive trial evidence; cognition/dementia does not (yet). Anxiety evidence is thinner still.
  • Time horizon. Mood can shift in weeks; dementia prevention plays out over decades, where trials are hardest to run.
  • Individual factors. Baseline homocysteine (for B-vitamins), caffeine sensitivity and sleep, and any alcohol use all shift the picture.

Practical takeaways

  • Eat the Mediterranean-style pattern (§06) — vegetables, fruit, legumes, whole grains, nuts, olive oil, fish — for the best-supported mood and brain payoff. No special "brain diet" is needed.
  • Cut ultra-processed foods and sugary/diet drinks, the foods most consistently linked to higher depression risk.
  • Eat fish or take EPA-predominant omega-3 as a reasonable adjunct if you have low mood — discuss with your clinician, not in place of one. Adjust: baseline 2 servings oily fish/week (≈250–500 mg EPA+DHA/day) for general intake; for a mood trial, ~1 g EPA/day from a ≥60% EPA supplement for 6–8 weeks as an add-on (≤1 g, no extra benefit above; ≤1 g and clinician-cleared if on blood thinners).
  • Keep blood sugar steady with fiber, protein, and minimally-processed carbs to smooth out energy and mood. Adjust: aim for the §06/§02 fiber and protein targets (≈14 g fiber/1,000 kcal; protein per §02/§20) and pair carbs with protein/fat rather than eating them alone.
  • Mind caffeine and alcohol: caffeine for daytime alertness only; treat alcohol as a depressant and sleep-disrupter, not a mood aid (§07). Adjust caffeine: ≤400 mg/day total, ≤200 mg (~3 mg/kg) per dose, last dose ~8 h before bed; ≤200 mg/day in pregnancy, lower (~100–200 mg) if anxious or caffeine-sensitive. Adjust alcohol: less is better, 0 with active depression/anxiety, sleep problems, CNS-active meds, or pregnancy; if you drink, stay within ≤2 drinks/day (men) / ≤1 (women) and not near bedtime.
  • Get professional help for clinical symptoms. Diet is the supportive layer, never the treatment, for depression, anxiety, or dementia (§05, §09).

Unsettled or debated

  • Is the diet–depression effect causal, and how big? SMILES and HELFIMED say a real adjunct effect exists; the 2025 Annals review says it is small and low-certainty. Both can be true.
  • Does any diet protect the aging brain? Strong, consistent cohort data versus a null MIND RCT (§06). The cognitive-protection claim is currently promising but unproven.
  • B-vitamins for cognition: a benefit may exist only in people with elevated homocysteine, but population-wide supplementation is not supported.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

19. Food Safety and Cooking Methods

Bottom line: The cooking method and dose — not the food — drive risk: favor gentle moist heat, aim for golden-not-charred, and own a thermometer for the acute risk; keep mercury, pesticides, and arsenic in proportion and don't let contaminant fear cut your fish or produce.

How you cook matters almost as much as what you cook. The same fish can be a near-ideal meal or a source of avoidable carcinogens depending on whether it's gently poached or charred black over a flame. This section covers two kinds of risk: the slow, chronic ones from cooking chemistry (heat-formed compounds, oxidized oils) and the acute one from foodborne pathogens. It also puts a few much-hyped "contaminants" — mercury, pesticides, arsenic, plastics — into honest proportion.

A theme runs through all of it: the dose and the method, not the food itself, usually matter. Grilling isn't off-limits; repeated charring is the problem. Fish is one of the healthiest foods available; a few high-mercury species during pregnancy are the exception. The goal is to lower real risks with a handful of habits — not to add anxiety to every meal.

The acute risk deserves top billing because it's the one that sends people to the hospital. The CDC estimates roughly 48 million foodborne illnesses, 128,000 hospitalizations, and 3,000 deaths in the US each year CDC, nearly all preventable with four simple practices and a thermometer.

Key evidence

High-heat cooking of meat forms HCAs and PAHs (moderate; mechanism strong, human-cancer link suggestive). When muscle meat — beef, pork, poultry, fish — is cooked at high temperature, two families of mutagens form. Heterocyclic amines (HCAs) arise when amino acids, sugars, and creatine react above ~300°F (150°C); polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) form when fat drips onto flames and the smoke deposits on the surface NCI. Both are mutagenic and carcinogenic in animals; human epidemiology links frequent well-done/charred meat to colorectal, pancreatic, and prostate cancer, though it can't fully separate the compounds from the meat itself (cross-ref §05, §14). The NCI's own mitigation advice is concrete: avoid direct flame, flip frequently, microwave-precook to cut grilling time, and discard charred portions NCI.

Marinating measurably reduces HCAs (moderate). Marinating for at least 30 minutes — especially in marinades rich in polyphenolic antioxidants from herbs and spices — has cut total HCA formation by 50–90% in controlled grilling studies Smith et al., J Food Sci 2008. The antioxidants suppress the free-radical chemistry that builds HCAs.

Adjust it — grilling-mitigation stack. Each habit is independent and roughly additive against HCA/PAH load: marinate ≥30 min in an herb/spice (rosemary, thyme, garlic, turmeric) or acidic base (−50% to −90% total HCAs, with rosemary extract alone >−90%); flip every ~1 min instead of once (frequent flipping substantially lowers HCAs by holding surface temperature down, per NCI); microwave-precook ~2 min and pour off the juices before grilling (≈−90% HCAs per NCI, by shedding grill time and the creatine/precursor pool); avoid flare-ups and trim dripping fat to limit PAHs; discard charred/blackened bits. Scaling: benefit tracks total high-char meat frequency — at ≤1×/week the absolute risk is small and one or two habits suffice; at daily char (e.g., habitual barbecue or pan-blackening) stack all of them. Depends on: higher-risk colorectal profile (family history, age >50, prior adenoma — cross-ref §09) → treat well-done/charred red meat as a several-×/month ceiling, not a daily food.

Starchy foods cooked dry above 120°C form acrylamide (moderate; IARC Group 2A "probable"). Frying, roasting, and baking starchy foods (potatoes, bread, crackers, coffee) above 120°C drives the Maillard reaction between asparagine and sugars, producing acrylamide — the browning chemistry that also makes food tasty. IARC rates it a probable human carcinogen, and EFSA concluded that dietary acrylamide potentially raises cancer risk for consumers across all age groups, though the human data are limited and animal-based EFSA 2015. Practical control: cook to golden, not brown; soak raw potatoes before frying; store potatoes outside the fridge (cold storage raises sugars and thus acrylamide).

Adjust it — acrylamide control. Baseline: the reaction effectively switches on above 120°C (250°F) and climbs steeply with browning, so "golden, not brown" is the single dial — visibly browner = exponentially more acrylamide. How it scales: soaking cut raw potato in water 15–30 min before frying/roasting (then drying, and frying only to light gold) cuts acrylamide ~40% (≈48% with a 2-hour soak); toasting bread to light-gold rather than dark cuts it severalfold; pantry (not fridge) potato storage avoids the cold-induced sugar rise. Depends on: children carry the highest exposure per kg body weight (EFSA) — go lighter on fries/crisps/over-toasted starches for kids; heavy daily fried-starch or dark-toast eaters get the most absolute benefit from dialing browning back; occasional golden fries are a non-issue.

Dry high heat creates dietary AGEs (limited–moderate). Advanced glycation end-products (AGEs) form when proteins and sugars react under dry heat; grilling, roasting, and frying can raise food AGE content 10- to 100-fold over the raw (uncooked) state, and several-fold over moist-cooked (boiled/steamed) food Uribarri et al., J Acad Nutr Diet 2010. Dietary AGEs are associated with oxidative stress and inflammation, and small trials show moist, lower-temperature cooking lowers circulating AGEs — the same lesson as the HCA and acrylamide story.

Adjust it — dietary AGEs. Baseline: the cooking method dominates over the food — broiling/grilling/frying generates roughly 10–100× the AGEs of boiling/steaming/poaching the same item, and animal foods high in fat and protein (fatty meats, full-fat cheese, butter) are the highest-AGE foods. How it scales: switching a dish from dry high-heat to moist low-heat, plus acidic marinades (lemon, vinegar), can cut its AGE load by roughly half or more. Depends on: this lever matters most for those with diabetes, CKD, or established cardiovascular disease (cross-ref §09), where lower dietary-AGE patterns have shown the clearest benefit; for metabolically healthy people the evidence is weaker (see Unsettled), so treat it as a free side-benefit of cooking gentler rather than a standalone mandate.

Gentle, moist methods reduce all three — and don't char. Steaming, boiling, stewing, poaching, sous-vide, and slow-cooking stay near or below water's boiling point, so they form far fewer HCAs, PAHs, acrylamide, and AGEs. This is the single highest-leverage cooking habit: favor moist, lower-temperature methods for everyday cooking and save searing/charring for occasional use.

Nutrient retention cuts both ways (moderate). Cooking isn't universally destructive. Heat ruptures plant cell walls and makes some nutrients more available — cooked tomato products deliver more absorbable lycopene than raw, and a little oil sharply boosts carotenoid (lycopene, beta-carotene, lutein) absorption Lycopene review, PMC 2021. The trade-off is water-soluble vitamins: in a head-to-head study, boiling was the harshest on vitamin C and microwaving the gentlest, with steaming in between — less water and shorter time preserve more Lee et al., PMC 2018. Microwaving, despite its reputation, is among the more nutrient-protective methods.

Adjust it — method should follow the food. Default lever: shorter time + less water + lower temperature retains the most heat-/water-labile nutrients; boiling can leach ~50%+ of vitamin C from greens, while microwaving/steaming the same vegetable typically retains far more. When to flip the rule: for carotenoid-rich foods (tomato, carrot, red pepper, leafy greens), light cooking plus ~3–5 g added fat raises usable lycopene/beta-carotene several-fold — heat and oil are a feature, not a cost, here. Depends on the target nutrient: maximizing vitamin C / folate / glucosinolates → steam or microwave, minimal water, eat soon; maximizing lycopene / beta-carotene / lutein → cook with a little oil; boiling water-soluble vitamins away is only "lost" if you discard the cooking water — keep it for soups/stocks to recover much of it.

Oils: respect smoke points and don't reuse oil repeatedly (moderate). Heating oil past its smoke point degrades it and produces irritants and PAHs; repeatedly reheated frying oil shifts its fatty-acid profile unfavorably — trans-fat content rises and atherogenicity/thrombogenicity indices worsen, a more cardiovascular-adverse fat — across successive frying cycles Effects of Repeated Heating, PMC 2022. Match oil smoke point to cooking heat, and avoid reusing the same oil more than a couple of times.

Adjust it — oil choice and reuse. Baseline: keep cooking temperature below the oil's smoke point, and reuse frying oil no more than 2–3 cycles, discarding sooner if it darkens, smells acrid, or foams. How it scales (match heat to smoke point): refined avocado ~270°C (520°F) and refined/light olive ~240°C (465°F) suit high-heat searing/deep-frying; extra-virgin olive ~190–210°C (375–410°F) and butter ~150°C (300°F) are for low-medium sauté/baking only; degradation markers (trans fats, polar compounds, atherogenicity index) climb with each reheat cycle. Depends on: deep-frying (oil reused across batches) → filter and cap at ~2–3 uses and never top-up old with new; a single sauté in fresh oil → reuse is a non-issue; dyslipidemia / established CVD (cross-ref §09) → minimize repeatedly-fried foods entirely, since the fat shifts toward a more atherogenic, trans-richer profile.

Foodborne illness: four practices plus safe temperatures (strong). The CDC/USDA framework is clean, separate, cook, chill FoodSafety.gov. A verified internal temperature is what actually kills pathogens: poultry 165°F (74°C); ground meats 160°F (71°C); whole beef, pork, veal, and lamb (steaks, roasts, chops) 145°F (63°C) with a 3-minute rest; fish and seafood 145°F (63°C) (or until opaque and flaking); egg dishes 160°F (71°C) FoodSafety.gov. Keep the fridge ≤40°F (4°C) and never leave perishable food in the 40–140°F "danger zone" over 2 hours (1 hour above 90°F) CDC. Refrigerate leftovers within that window, use them within 3–4 days, and reheat to 165°F (74°C) (bring soups, sauces, and gravies to a rolling boil) FSIS. Higher-risk foods include raw/undercooked poultry, eggs, and seafood, unpasteurized milk/juice, raw sprouts, and deli meats; higher-risk people are pregnant women, young children, adults over 65, and the immunocompromised.

Adjust it — safe temperatures and timing. - Baseline (healthy adults): thermometer targets above; danger-zone clock 2 h in the 40–140°F zone, 1 h when ambient is >90°F (32°C); leftovers 3–4 days at ≤40°F (4°C). - How it scales: pathogen kill is a time–temperature trade-off, so a target a few degrees low just needs hold time, not panic — a 7-log Salmonella reduction is reached instantly at 165°F (74°C) but also at lower temperatures held longer (FSIS publishes validated lower-temperature/longer-hold combinations down to ~130°F for whole-muscle product) FSIS Appendix A. Treat 165°F as the no-timing-required shortcut for home cooks. Cold storage buys time the other way: bacterial growth slows sharply as you move below 40°F, which is why the fridge target is a ceiling, not a goal. - What it depends on (numeric): pregnant / age >65 / immunocompromised → treat the danger-zone clock as half (≤1 h at room temp), eat leftovers within 1–2 days, and reheat deli meat and hot dogs to steaming 165°F to kill Listeria; >90°F/32°C ambient (summer picnic, hot car) → 1 h not 2 h; freezer storage extends quality (frozen food stays safe indefinitely at 0°F, but quality degrades) — use most cooked leftovers within 3–4 months of freezing; whole-cut beef/pork rare-preference is acceptable only at 145°F + 3-min rest (the rest delivers further log-reduction), but ground meat has no safe "rare" — surface pathogens are mixed throughout, so 160°F is non-negotiable.

Contaminants, in proportion:

  • Mercury in fish. Most fish are net-beneficial; a few predators concentrate methylmercury. FDA/EPA advise pregnant and breastfeeding people to eat 8–12 oz/week (2–3 servings of 4 oz) from the "Best Choices" list (salmon, sardines, shrimp, pollock, canned light tuna, tilapia, trout, catfish, Atlantic mackerel, anchovies, herring) — or 1 serving/week of a "Good Choices" fish (e.g., albacore/yellowfin tuna, halibut, mahi-mahi) and nothing else that week — while avoiding the 7 highest-mercury species: shark, swordfish, king mackerel, tilefish (Gulf of Mexico), marlin, orange roughy, and bigeye tuna FDA/EPA (cross-ref §04, §16).

Adjust it — fish servings by group. - General healthy adults: 2–3 servings/week (≈8–12 oz total), emphasizing low-mercury, omega-3-rich choices (salmon, sardines, herring); no upper "avoid" list applies but keep high-mercury predators to ≈1 serving/month at most. - Pregnant / breastfeeding / planning pregnancy: hold to 8–12 oz/week of Best Choices, strictly zero of the 7 avoid-species (methylmercury crosses the placenta and concentrates in the fetus), and cap albacore ("white") tuna at ≤6 oz/week. - Children, by age (serving size scales with body size): 1–3 yr → 1 oz per serving; 4–7 yr → 2 oz; 8–10 yr → 3 oz; 11+ → 4 oz (adult); 2 servings/week from Best Choices. - Local/recreational catch: if no advisory exists, limit to 1 serving (4 oz)/week and eat no other fish that week (waterbody mercury varies 10–100×).

  • Pesticide residues. Regulators consistently find the large majority of sampled produce below safety tolerances, and EFSA and FDA both conclude the benefits of eating fruits and vegetables far outweigh residue risk EFSA. Washing under running water reduces residues; don't let residue fear cut your produce intake.

Adjust it — pesticide residues. Baseline: rinse all produce 20–30 sec under running water (rubbing firm produce) before eating — this removes a meaningful fraction of surface residue and dirt/microbes; soap and commercial "produce washes" are unnecessary and not FDA-recommended. How it scales / depends on: the net health benefit of eating more fruits and vegetables far outweighs residue risk at any realistic intake, so the priority order is quantity first, sourcing second. If budget is limited and you want to spend it, prioritize organic only for the few thin-skinned high-residue items (e.g., berries, leafy greens, peppers — the EWG "Dirty Dozen" pattern) and skip it for thick-skinned/low-residue produce (avocado, onion, citrus); for pregnancy/young children the same rule holds — washing plus variety beats fear-driven avoidance.

  • Arsenic in rice. Rice takes up inorganic arsenic from soil. Cooking it in excess water (6–10 parts water to 1 part rice) then draining removes 40–60% of the inorganic arsenic (≈40% for polished long-grain, ≈50% for brown, ≈60% for parboiled), whereas rinsing alone has minimal effect on arsenic, per the FDA; the trade-off is that draining also strips 50–70% of the enriched iron, folate, niacin, and thiamin from polished/parboiled rice FDA. Varying grains (oats, barley, quinoa) lowers exposure, which matters most for infants — the FDA's action level for inorganic arsenic in infant rice cereal is 100 ppb FDA.

Adjust it — rice and arsenic. - Baseline (average adult, varied diet): rice once or twice a week needs no special handling; the excess-water-and-drain method is optional. - How it scales: exposure is roughly linear in rice frequency × per-serving arsenic; brown rice runs ~80% higher in inorganic arsenic than white (arsenic concentrates in the bran), and parboiled/excess-water cooking cuts it most (≈60%). - What it depends on (numeric): infants/toddlers on rice cereal → choose products at/under the 100 ppb action level and rotate to oat/barley cereals (brain-development window, low body weight); frequent rice eaters (≥1×/day) or rice-heavy diets (some South/East Asian patterns) → use the 6–10:1 drain method and favor lower-arsenic varieties (California white basmati and US sushi rice run ~50% lower; non-basmati brown and rice grown in AR/LA/TX run highest); pregnancy → cap brown-rice frequency and diversify grains. If you drain-cook to lower arsenic, take folate/iron from other foods or a supplement to offset the 50–70% nutrient loss.

  • Plastics/BPA and nitrates (brief, non-alarmist). Avoid microwaving food in plastic and prefer glass/stainless for hot or fatty foods; regulators consider typical exposures low-risk, but the precaution is cheap. Dietary nitrate from vegetables is not the same risk as nitrites in processed meat (cross-ref §05) — vegetable nitrate appears largely benign.

What it depends on

  • Who's eating. Pregnancy, infancy, older age, and immunosuppression raise the stakes on both pathogens and mercury/arsenic; healthy adults have much more margin (cross-ref §16).
  • Frequency. Occasional grilling or fried food is trivial; daily charred meat or daily reused-oil frying is where chronic risk accrues.
  • The food. Cooking helps tomatoes and carrots but costs vitamin C in greens — method should follow the food.
  • Baseline diet quality. These are refinements on top of the big levers (§04, §05), not substitutes for them.

Practical takeaways

  • Cook gentler by default: steam, boil, stew, poach, or slow-cook for everyday meals; reserve high char for occasional use, and scrape off blackened bits.
  • When you grill or fry meat: marinate first, flip often, avoid open flame and flare-ups, and consider microwave pre-cooking to shorten grill time.
  • Aim for golden, not brown on potatoes, toast, and baked starches; store potatoes in the pantry, not the fridge.
  • Buy a $10 food thermometer and hit 165°F poultry / 160°F ground meat (and egg dishes) / 145°F + 3-min rest for whole cuts and fish; keep the fridge ≤40°F, follow the 2-hour rule (1 hour if >90°F), eat leftovers within 3–4 days, and reheat them to 165°F. High-risk eaters: halve the danger-zone clock and use leftovers within 1–2 days.
  • Don't reuse frying oil more than 2–3 times; match oil smoke point to cooking heat (high heat → refined avocado/light olive; low heat → EVOO/butter).
  • Eat fish 2–3×/week (≈8–12 oz), favoring low-mercury, omega-3-rich choices; in pregnancy/breastfeeding hold to 8–12 oz/week of Best Choices and strictly avoid the 7 high-mercury species; children's servings scale by age (1 oz at 1–3 yr up to 4 oz at 11+).
  • Wash produce, skip the fear — pesticide residues don't justify eating fewer fruits and vegetables. Cook rice in excess water and vary your grains.

Unsettled or debated

  • How much heat-formed compounds matter at real-world doses. The mechanisms (HCAs, PAHs, acrylamide, AGEs) are solid, but quantifying how much typical home cooking raises an individual's cancer risk is genuinely uncertain — the human epidemiology is observational and confounded by overall meat and diet patterns (cross-ref §14).
  • Dietary AGEs as an independent risk factor. Whether food AGEs (versus those the body forms internally from high blood sugar) meaningfully drive disease in humans is still actively debated, with mostly short-term trial evidence.

Last reviewed: June 2026.

🥦 Fullness-per-calorie score (your satiety algorithm)

Score any food by how full it leaves you per calorie — the quantity that actually governs appetite and weight. Enter the nutrition facts per 100 g. Whole-fruit and plain-dairy sugar isn’t penalized — only added/free sugar is.

TierScoreMeaning
5 ●●●●●≥ 3.50Very high — fills you up on almost no calories (most veg, broth, white fish, berries)
4 ●●●●○2.60–3.49High — very satiating per calorie (legumes, eggs, lean meat, yogurt, oats, potatoes, fruit)
3 ●●●○○1.80–2.59Moderate (pasta, rice, bread, cheese, nuts, avocado)
2 ●●○○○1.15–1.79Low — many calories before you feel full (bagels, granola, soda, juice, ice cream)
1 ●○○○○< 1.15Very low — calorie-dense, barely filling (oil, butter, chips, chocolate)

Implements the open fullness-per-calorie algorithm (energy density + protein + fiber − fat − added sugar, with form and processing modifiers; white bread anchors the Tier 2/3 border). A guide to relative satiety per calorie, not a calorie counter or a verdict on any one food.
